Register Set Automata (Technical Report)
Sabína Gulčíková, Ondřej Lengál

Abstract
We present register set automata ( RsA \mathrm{RsA} s), a register automaton model over data words where registers can contain sets of data values and the following operations are supported: adding values to registers, clearing registers, and testing (non-)membership. We show that the emptiness problem for RsA \mathrm{RsA} s is decidable and complete for the 𝐅 ω \mathbf{F}_{\omega} class. Moreover, we show that a large class of register automata can be transformed into deterministic RsA \mathrm{RsA} s, which can serve as a basis for (i) fast matching of a family of regular expressions with back-references and (ii) language inclusion algorithm for a sub-class of register automata. RsA \mathrm{RsA} s are incomparable in expressive power to other popular automata models over data words, such as alternating register automata and pebble automata.

Introduction

Automata over infinite alphabets have found applications in many areas of
computer science, for instance in
modelling network protocols
[
5
]
,
reasoning about XML (XML schema definition via XSD, XML transformation via
XSLT)
[
30
]
,
pattern matching with back-references
[
37
]
,
runtime verification
[
21
]
,
verification of MapReduce programs
[
8
,
7
]
, or even
testing satisfiability in the SMT theory of strings
[
6
]
.

A popular formal model for languages over infinite alphabets are the so-called
register automata.
(Nondeterministic)
register automata
(RAs,
\mathrm{NRA}
s) are
an extension of (nondeterministic) finite automata (FAs, NFAs) equipped with a finite
set of registers, each of which can store a single value copied from the input
tape (which contains symbols from an infinite data domain
\mathbb{D}
).
On transitions, the automata can then test (non-)equality of the current input symbol
and values stored inside the registers, and save the input symbol into some of
the registers.
Compared to NFAs, the complexity of decision problems for
\mathrm{NRA}
s is much
higher, for example, language emptiness, which is solvable in linear time for
NFAs, is
Pspace
-complete for
\mathrm{NRA}
s.
Furthermore, universality (“
Does an automaton accept all words?
”),
language inclusion, and equivalence, which is
Pspace
-complete for NFAs, are,
in general,
undecidable for
\mathrm{NRA}
s
[
31
]
.
The models also significantly differ in their expressive power: NFAs can
be easily determinised but
\mathrm{NRA}
s cannot.
For instance, the language of strings containing two occurrences of
the same data value can be
accepted by an NRA but by no
\mathrm{DRA}
.

In this paper, we study
register set automata
(
\mathrm{RsA}
s), a formal model that
extends RAs with the possibility to store
sets of data values
into
registers.
1
1

1

The
\mathrm{RsA}
model is inspired by the model of
counting set automata
of Turoňová

et al.

[
47
]
, which allow compact deterministic
representation of a subclass of NFAs extended with bounded counters.

Our study of the model is motivated by the fact that
deterministic

\mathrm{RsA}
s
can capture a quite large fragment of
\mathrm{NRA}
languages.
The need for deterministic automata models comes (not only) from
two applications:

(i)

regex matching with back-references and

(ii)

checking language inclusion or equivalence of
\mathrm{NRA}
s,

which are both described in more detail below.

Matching Regexes with Back-references.

Pattern matching using regular expressions with
back-references
is performed ubiquitously, e.g., in validating user inputs on web
pages, processing text using the
grep
and
sed
tools,
transforming XML documents, or detecting network
incidents
[
35
,
3
]
.
Consider, for instance, the (extended) regular expression (regex)

R_{\texttt{\textbackslash 3\textbackslash 2\textbackslash 1}}={}

/(.).*;.*(.).*;.*(.).*\3\2\1/
,

where we use
the wildcard
"."
to denote any symbol except the semicolon
";"

(i.e., it stands for the character class
"[^;]"
),
parentheses
"(
…
)"
to denote the so-called

capture groups
, and
"\x"
to denote
a
back-reference
to the string captured by the
x
’th capture
group.
Intuitively, the regex matches input strings
w
that can be seen as
a concatenation of three strings
w=uvz
such that
u
has the structure

u=u_{1}\,\mathtt{;}\,u_{2}\,\mathtt{;}\,u_{3}
with
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\in(\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{;}\})^{+}
,
v
is a string of three characters
a_{3}a_{2}a_{1}

such that
a_{i}\in u_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, and
z\in(\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{;}\})^{*}
(such a regex can describe, e.g., a simplified version of the
match rule of some XML transformation).
Trying to find a match of the regex in the randomly generated 42-character-long string

"ah;jk2367ash;la5akv45lwkjb9f.dj5fqkbxsfyrf"

using the state-of-the-art PCRE2 regex matcher available at

regex101.com

[
33
]
takes
10,416 steps before reporting
no match
.
2
2

2

Finding a match of the regex

/(.).*(.).*(.).*\3\2\1/
in the same text took 179,372 steps before
no match
was
reported.
The regex is more challenging—it does not use delimiters (semicolons in

R_{\texttt{\textbackslash 3\textbackslash 2\textbackslash 1}}
).

Ideally, the matcher should take only 42 steps, one step for every
character in the string.
This
\frac{10,416}{42}=248\times
slowdown is caused by the so-called

catastrophic backtracking
—the PCRE2 matcher is based on backtracking
and, since the regex is nondeterministic, the backtracking algorithm needs to
try all possibilities of placing the three capture groups before concluding that
there is no match.

If such a scenario with catastrophic backtracking happens, e.g., in a web
application by providing a malicious user input tailored to take advantage of
a feature of a regex that makes it perform poorly, this can have serious
consequences: the web may become nonresponsive and unusable.
In industry, this is called a
regular expression denial of
service
(ReDoS) attack
[
1
]
.
ReDoS is a real-world threat; it caused, for instance,
the 2016 outage of StackOverflow
[
15
]
or rendered
vulnerable websites that were using the Express.js framework
[
2
]
.

A practical way how to avoid poor performance of regex matchers is to use
a matching algorithm with no backtracking.
Indeed, the majority of industrial-strength regex matchers avoid backtracking by
using a matching algorithm based on
deterministic
FAs (DFAs), which
guarantee constant per-symbol matching time
3
3

3

In
practice, the matchers are based on Thompson’s algorithm
[
45
]
,
which does not build the (possibly prohibitively large) DFA

a priori
but, instead, on the fly, while using cache to store already
constructed parts of the DFA.
This gives almost constant per-symbol matching time for usual
inputs if the cache is utilized well, malicious inputs can, however, cause high
cache miss ratio and performance degradation.
.
Such algorithms are, however, not available when back-references are used,
witnessed by the fact that neither of the two currently most advanced regex
matchers in the industry, RE2
[
19
]
and HyperScan
[
48
]
,
supports back-references, due to a missing efficient expressive
deterministic automaton
model
[
23
,
20
]
.

Language Inclusion of Register Automata.

Testing RA inclusion is an essential problem in many of their applications, such as
in their minimization,
learning
[
5
,
25
,
18
]
,
checking for fixpoint in regular model checking
[
6
]
,
checking XML schema subsumption
[
46
]
,
verification of parameterized concurrent programs with shared
memory
[
24
]
, and is an essential component of the RA toolkit.
Unfortunately, inclusion of RAs is in general an undecidable problem
[
31
]
, which
has forced researchers to either find ways to approximate the inclusion
test—e.g., via several membership
tests
[
25
,
18
]
or by an abstraction refinement
semi-algorithm using interpolation
[
24
]
—or restrict themselves to
other models with decidable inclusion problem, such as deterministic RAs (whose
expressive power is quite limited)
[
28
]

or session automata
[
5
]
.

The standard approach to testing the language inclusion
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B})
is to

(i)

determinise
\mathcal{B}
into
\mathcal{B}^{D}
,

(ii)

complement
\mathcal{B}^{D}
into
\overline{\mathcal{B}^{D}}
, and

(iii)

test emptiness of the intersection of
\mathcal{A}

and
\overline{\mathcal{B}^{D}}
.

Here, the critical point is already the
determinisation
part: RAs cannot
always be determinised
(and it is undecidable whether a particular
RA
can be determinised

[
9
,
10
]
).

The previous two applications show a strong need for expressive deterministic
models of automata with registers.
We address this need by introducing
register set automata
(
\mathrm{RsA}
s), an
extension of RAs where every register can hold a
set of
values
instead of a single one.
This model is quite powerful: it strictly generalizes RAs and is incomparable to
(one-way) alternating RAs, another popular powerful generalization of RAs.
The core property of
\mathrm{RsA}
s is that their emptiness problem is decidable,
although for a higher price than for RAs—the complexity blows from

Pspace
-complete for RAs to
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-complete (i.e., Ackermannian) for

\mathrm{RsA}
s.
Furthermore,
\mathrm{RsA}
s are practically relevant: many
\mathrm{NRA}
s (e.g., the
\mathrm{NRA}

for the regex
R_{\texttt{\textbackslash 3\textbackslash 2\textbackslash 1}}
) can be algorithmically determinised into

deterministic

\mathrm{RsA}
s (
\mathrm{DRsA}
s), which, together with the emptiness
decidability, gives us an algorithm for testing language inclusion of
a large class of RAs (the last missing component—complementing a
\mathrm{DRsA}
—is
done easily by swapping final and non-final states).

Contribution.

We list the main contributions of the paper:

1.

introduction of the model of register set automata and showing its closure
properties,

2.

proving
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-completeness of the emptiness problem for
\mathrm{RsA}
s by
showing interreducibility with the coverability problem for transfer Petri
nets,

3.

designing a (semi-)algorithm that can determinise an RA into
a
\mathrm{DRsA}
and showing that it is complete for the class of languages that can
be obtained from RAs with one register and no disequality tests by
Boolean operations (i.e., union, intersection, and complement),

4.

placing the
\mathrm{RsA}
model into the landscape of automata-with-registers
models, and

5.

discussing the power of several extensions of the model.

2
Preliminaries

We use
{\rm Nature}
to denote the set of natural numbers without 0,
{\rm Nature}_{0}
to denote

{\rm Nature}\cup\{0\}
, and
[n]
for
n\in{\rm Nature}
to denote the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
(we note that
[0]=\emptyset
).
We sometimes use “
\cdot
” to denote an
ellipsis
, i.e., a value that
can be ignored.

Data Words.

Let us fix a finite nonempty
alphabet

\Sigma
and an infinite

data domain

\mathbb{D}
. A (finite)
data word
of
length

n
is a function
w\colon[n]\to(\Sigma\times\mathbb{D})
; we use
|w|=n
to denote its length
and
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
to denote its symbols.
The
empty word
of length 0 is denoted
\epsilon
.
We use
\Sigma[w]
and
\mathbb{D}[w]
to denote the

projection
of
w
onto the respective domain (e.g., if
w=\langle a,1\rangle\langle b,2\rangle\langle b,3\rangle
, then
\Sigma[w]=abc
and

\mathbb{D}[w]=123
) and, given
a\in\Sigma
, we use
a[w_{i}]

as a shortcut for
\Sigma[w_{i}]=a
.

Register Automata on Data Words.

A (nondeterministic one-way)
register automaton
(on data words),
abbreviated as (N)RA, is a tuple
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta,I,F)
where

Q
is a finite set of
states
,

\mathbf{R}
is a finite set of
registers
,

I\subseteq Q
is a set of
initial states
,

F\subseteq Q
is a set of
final states
, and

\Delta\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times(\mathbf{R}\to\mathbf{R}\cup\{\mathit{in},\bot\})\times Q
is a
transition relation
such that if

t\colon(q,a,g^{=},g^{\neq},\mathit{up},s)\in\Delta
, then
g^{=}\cap g^{\neq}=\emptyset
.
We use
q\text{\small{\hbox to69.98pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.99156pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}s
to denote
t
(and often
drop from
\mathit{up}
mappings
r\mapsto r
for
r\in\mathbf{R}
, which we treat as
implicit).
The semantics of
t
is that
\mathcal{A}
can move from state
q
to state
s
if the

\Sigma
-symbol at the current position of the input word is
a
and the

\mathbb{D}
-value at the current position is equal to all registers
from
g^{=}
and not equal to any register from
g^{\neq}
; the content of
the registers is updated so that
r_{i}\leftarrow\mathit{up}(r_{i})
(i.e.,
r_{i}
can be
assigned the value of some other register, the current
\mathbb{D}
-symbol,
denoted by
\mathit{in}
, or it can be cleared by being assigned
\bot
).

A
configuration
of
\mathcal{A}
is a pair
c\in Q\times(\mathbf{R}\to\mathbb{D}\cup\{\bot\})
, i.e., it consists of a state and an assignment of data values
to registers.
An
initial configuration
of
\mathcal{A}
is a pair
c_{\mathit{init}}\in I\times\{\{r\mapsto\bot\mid r\in\mathbf{R}\}\}
.
Suppose
c_{1}=(q_{1},f_{1})
and
c_{2}=(q_{2},f_{2})
are two configurations of
\mathcal{A}
.
We say that
c_{1}
can make a
step
to
c_{2}
over
\langle a,d\rangle\in\Sigma\times\mathbb{D}
using transition
t\colon q\text{\small{\hbox to69.98pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.99156pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

, denoted as

c_{1}\mathrel{\vdash^{\langle a,d\rangle}_{t}}c_{2}
, iff

1.

d=f_{1}(r)
for all
r\in g^{=}
,

2.

d\neq f_{1}(r)
for all
r\in g^{\neq}
, and

3.

for all

, we have

f_{2}(r)=\begin{cases}f_{1}(r^{\prime})&\text{if }\mathit{up}(r)=r^{\prime}\in\mathbf{R},\\
d&\text{if }\mathit{up}(r)=\mathit{in},\text{ and}\\
\bot&\text{if }\mathit{up}(r)=\bot.\end{cases}

A
run

\rho
of
\mathcal{A}
over the word
w=\langle a_{1},d_{1}\rangle\ldots\langle a_{n},d_{n}\rangle
from a configuration
c
is a sequence of
alternating configurations and transitions
\rho=c_{0}t_{1}c_{1}t_{2}\ldots t_{n}c_{n}
such that
\forall 1\leq i\leq n\colon c_{i-1}\mathrel{\vdash^{\langle a_{i},d_{i}\rangle}_{t_{i}}}c_{i}
and
c_{0}=c
.
We say that
\rho
is accepting if
c
is an initial configuration,
c_{n}=(s,f)
, and
s\in F
.
The language accepted by
\mathcal{A}
, denoted as
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
, is defined as

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})=\{w\in(\Sigma\times\mathbb{D})^{*}\mid\mathcal{A}\text{ has an
accepting run over }w\}
.

We say that
\mathcal{A}
is a
deterministic RA

\mathrm{DRA}

Universal RAs.

A
universal RA
(
\mathrm{URA}
)
\mathcal{A}_{U}
is defined exactly as an
\mathrm{NRA}
with
the exception of its language.
The language of
\mathcal{A}_{U}
is the set
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{U})=\{w\in(\Sigma\times\mathbb{D})^{*}\mid
every run of
\mathcal{A}_{U}
on
w
is
accepting
\}
(we emphasize that if a run cannot continue from some state over
the current input symbol, it is not accepting).

There is indeed duality between
\mathrm{NRA}
s and
\mathrm{URA}
s, as stated by the following
fact.

Fact 1
.

For every
\mathrm{NRA}

\mathcal{A}_{N}
, there is a
\mathrm{URA}
accepting the complement
of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{N})
.Conversely, for every
\mathrm{URA}

\mathcal{A}_{U}
, there is an
\mathrm{NRA}
accepting the
complement of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{U})
.

Proof.

For both parts, the complement automaton is obtained by

(i)

adding a rejecting
sink
state to the automaton,

(ii)

completing the transition relation (i.e., adding transitions for
undefined combinations of symbols and guards to the sink state) of the input automaton, and

(iii)

swapping final and non-final states.

∎

Example 2
.

Consider the language of words over
\Sigma=\{a\}
that contain two
occurrences of some data value, i.e., the language

L_{\exists\mathit{repeat}}=\{w\mid\exists i,j\colon i\neq j\land\mathbb{D}[w_{i}]=\mathbb{D}[w_{j}]\}.

An
\mathrm{NRA}
recognising this language is in the following figure:
q
s
t
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r\leftarrow\mathit{in}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\neq r\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}=r\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}

Formally, it is an
\mathrm{NRA}

\mathcal{A}=(\{q,s,t\},\{r\},\Delta,\{q\},\{t\})

with
\Delta=\{q\text{\small{\hbox to73.41pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 36.70323pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 34.75 12.28 L -34.75 12.28 C -38.19 12.28 -40.98 9.49 -40.98 6.05 L -40.98 0.81 C -40.98 -2.63 -38.19 -5.42 -34.75 -5.42 L 34.75 -5.42 C 38.19 -5.42 40.98 -2.63 40.98 0.81 L 40.98 6.05 C 40.98 9.49 38.19 12.28 34.75 12.28 Z M -40.98 -5.42}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-26.91269pt}{-0.03874pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{69}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

(actually, the guard
\mathit{in}\neq r
on the self-loop over
s
is redundant).
We note that this language is not expressible by any
\mathrm{DRA}
or
\mathrm{URA}
.
Intuitively,
\mathcal{A}
waits in
q
until it nondeterministically guesses the input
data value that should be repeated, stores it into register
r
, and moves to
state
s
.
In state
s
, it is waiting to see the data value again, upon which it moves to
the accepting state
t
and reads out the rest of the word.

On the other hand, the complement of the language, i.e., the language of words
where no two positions have the same data value, formally,

L_{\neg\exists\mathit{repeat}}=\{w\mid\forall i,j\colon i\neq j\implies\mathbb{D}[w_{i}]\neq\mathbb{D}[w_{j}]\},

is not expressible by any
\mathrm{DRA}
or
\mathrm{NRA}
, but is expressible by a
\mathrm{URA}
.
The
\mathrm{URA}
accepting
L_{\neg\exists\mathit{repeat}}
looks similar to the
\mathrm{NRA}
above with the
exception of final states, which are
\{q,s\}
(note that in
\mathrm{URA}
s, in order
to accept a word, all runs over the word need to accept, so in order to
accept in this example, all runs of the
\mathrm{URA}
need to avoid the state
t
).
∎

Alternating RAs.

Intuitively,
alternating RAs
(
\mathrm{ARA}
s) allow to combine the accepting
conditions of
\mathrm{NRA}
s and
\mathrm{URA}
s in a fine-grained way (states are marked

existential
or
universal
to denote whether, in order to accept
from the state,
at least one
run or
all
runs respectively need to accept).
Since in this paper we do not explicitly work with
\mathrm{ARA}
s and only compare to

\mathrm{ARA}
s in their expressive power, we avoid giving their quite involved formal
definition and point the interested reader to the definition of one-way
\mathrm{ARA}
s in
the paper of Demri and Lazić
[
12
]
.

Alternating RAs with
\mathsf{guess}
and
\mathsf{spread}
.

\mathrm{ARA}
s can also be extended with the two following operations:
\mathsf{guess}
and

\mathsf{spread}
.
The
\mathsf{guess}
operation allows a thread to assign a nondeterministically chosen
data value into some register (in each thread a different one).
On the other hand, the
\mathsf{spread}
operation allows to make a certain kind of
universal quantification over the data values seen so far on the run of the
automaton (even in different threads).

\mathrm{ARA}
s extended with both these operations are denoted as

\mathrm{ARA}(\mathsf{g,s})
. As with
\mathrm{ARA}
s, we do not directly work with this extension and only compare
with its expressive power.
The interested reader can find the formal definition in the paper of
Figueira
[
16
]
.

We use
\mathrm{NRA}^{=}
,
\mathrm{URA}^{=}
, and
\mathrm{DRA}^{=}
to denote the sub-classes of
\mathrm{NRA}
s,

\mathrm{URA}
s, and
\mathrm{DRA}
s
with
no disequality
guards, i.e., automata where for every transition

q\text{\small{\hbox to69.98pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.99156pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

it holds that
g^{\neq}=\emptyset
.
Furthermore,
for a class
\mathcal{C}
of automata with registers and
n\in{\rm Nature}
, we
use
\mathcal{C}_{n}
to denote the sub-class of
\mathcal{C}
containing automata
with at most
n
registers (e.g.,
\mathrm{DRA}_{2}
).
We abuse notation and use
\mathcal{C}
to also denote the class of
languages defined by
\mathcal{C}
.

3
Register Set Automata

A (nondeterministic)
register set automaton
(on data words),
abbreviated as (N)
\mathrm{RsA}
is a tuple
\mathcal{A}_{S}=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta,I,F)
where

Q,\mathbf{R},I,F
are the same as for RAs and

\Delta\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times(\mathbf{R}\to 2^{\mathbf{R}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}})\times Q

such that
if

(as with

for

when defining

).

is
that
\mathcal{A}_{S}
can move from state
q
to state
s
if the
\Sigma
-symbol at
the current position of the input word is
a
and the
\mathbb{D}
-value at the
current position is in all registers from
g^{\in}
and in no register
from
g^{\notin}
; the content of the
registers is updated so that
r_{i}\leftarrow\bigcup\{x\mid x\in\mathit{up}(r_{i})\}

(i.e.,
r_{i}
can be assigned the union of values of several registers, possibly
including the current
\mathbb{D}
-symbol denoted by
\mathit{in}
).

A
configuration
of
\mathcal{A}_{S}
is a pair
c\in Q\times(\mathbf{R}\to 2^{\mathbb{D}})
, i.e., it consists of a state and an assignment of sets of data values
to registers.
An
initial configuration
of
\mathcal{A}_{S}
is a pair
c_{\mathit{init}}\in I\times\{\{r\mapsto\emptyset\mid r\in\mathbf{R}\}\}
.
Suppose
c_{1}=(q_{1},f_{1})
and
c_{2}=(q_{2},f_{2})
are two configurations of
\mathcal{A}_{S}
.
We say that
c_{1}
can make a
step
to
c_{2}
over
\langle a,d\rangle\in\Sigma\times\mathbb{D}
using transition

for

{{{{{}}{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}s\}
.
Intuitively, the
\mathrm{DRsA}
waits in
q
and accumulates the so-far seen
input data values in register
r
(we use
r\leftarrow r\cup\{\mathit{in}\}
to denote
the update
r\mapsto\{r,\mathit{in}\}
).
Once the
\mathrm{DRsA}
reads a value that is already in
r
, it moves to
s
and
accepts.
∎

Example 4
.

A
\mathrm{DRsA}_{1}
accepting the language
L_{\neg\exists\mathit{repeat}}
from
\cref
ex:ra_example is in

\cref
fig:drsa_langnegexrep.
Intuitively, the automaton stays in state
q
and accumulates input data values
in register
r
, making sure the input data value has not been seen
previously.
∎

Example 5
.

Consider the following language:

L_{\neg\forall\mathit{repeat}}=\{w\mid\exists i\forall j\colon i\neq j\implies\mathbb{D}[w_{i}]\neq\mathbb{D}[w_{j}]\}

Intuitively,
L_{\neg\forall\mathit{repeat}}
is the language of all words containing
a data value with exactly one occurrence.
This language is accepted, e.g., by the
\mathrm{RsA}_{1}
in

\cref
fig:rsa_langnegallrep.
The
\mathrm{RsA}
stays in state
q
, collecting the seen values into its
register, and at some point, when it sees a value not seen previously,
it nondeterministically moves to
s
, remembering the value in its
register.
Then, at state
s
, the
\mathrm{RsA}
just checks that it does not see the remembered
value any more.
∎

4
Properties of Register Set Automata

In this section, we establish decidability of basic decision problems
for
\mathrm{RsA}
s and their closure properties.
First, we claim that
\mathrm{RsA}
s generalise
\mathrm{NRA}
s.

Fact 6
.

For every
n\in{\rm Nature}
and
\mathrm{NRA}_{n}
, there exists an
\mathrm{RsA}_{n}
accepting the
same language.

The next theorem shows the core property of
\mathrm{RsA}
s: that their emptiness
problem is decidable, however, for
a much higher price than for
\mathrm{NRA}
s, for which it is

Pspace
-complete
4
4

4

Note that for an alternative definition of
\mathrm{NRA}
s considered
in
[
26
,
36
]
, where no two registers can contain the same data
value, the problem is
NP
-complete
[
36
]
.

[
12
]
.
For classifying the complexity of the problem, we use the hierarchy of
fast-growing complexity classes of Schmitz
[
38
]
, in particular the
class
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
, which, intuitively, corresponds to Ackermannian problems closed
under primitive-recursive reductions.

Theorem 7
.

The emptiness problem for
\mathrm{RsA}
is decidable,
in particular,
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-complete.

Sketch of proof.

The proof is done by showing interreducibility of
\mathrm{RsA}
emptiness with
coverability in
transfer Petri nets
(TPNs) (often used for modelling the
so-called
broadcast protocols
), which is a known
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-complete
problem
[
41
,
39
,
40
]
.
In the following, we briefly describe both directions
of the reduction (see
\cref
sec:proof-rsa-emptiness for details and examples).

(
\mathrm{RsA}
emptiness
\leq
TPN coverability)

Intuitively, the conversion of an
\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}

=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta,I,F)
into a TPN
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}
is done in the following way.
The set of places of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}
will be as follows:

(i)

one place for each state of
\mathcal{A}
,

(ii)

two special places
\mathit{init}
and
\mathit{fin}
, and

(iii)

one place for every subset
\mathit{rgn}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
; these places
are used to represent all possible intersections of values held in the
registers.
For instance, if there are four tokens in the place representing
r_{1}\cap r_{2}
, it means that there are exactly four different data values
stored in both
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
and in no other register.

Each transition
t
of
\mathcal{A}
is simulated by one or more TPN transitions between places
representing its source and target states.
The number of respective TPN transitions
depends on how specific the guard is in the original automaton, since we need to
distinguish every possible option of
\mathit{in}
being in some region
\mathit{rgn}\in 2^{\mathbf{R}}
.
The transitions move the token between the places corresponding to
t
’s
source and target states and, moreover, use the
broadcast
arcs to
move tokens between the places representing regions, according to the
manipulation of the set-registers in the update function of
t
.
The special place
\mathit{init}
is used to have a single starting marking
(it just nondeterministically chooses one state from
I
) and the place

\mathit{fin}
is used as the target for the coverability test; all places
corresponding to final states of
\mathcal{A}
can simply transition into it.

(TPN coverability
\leq

\mathrm{RsA}
emptiness)

Given a TPN
\mathcal{N}
, the
\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}

simulating it will have the following structure.
There will be a state
q_{\mathit{main}}
, which will be active before and
after the simulation of firing each transition of
\mathcal{N}
.
Moreover, there will be one register for every place of
\mathcal{N}
;
individual tokens in the places will be simulated by unique data values
from
\mathbb{D}
stored in the corresponding registers.
For each transition of
\mathcal{N}
, there will be a
gadget
, doing a cycle
on
q_{\mathit{main}}
, that represents the semantics of
\mathcal{N}
’s transition.
Each such gadget is composed of several
protogadgets
, which simulate
basic actions performed during the transition (adding a token to a place,
removing a token, moving all tokens between places).
Implementation of adding a token and moving tokens is relatively easy,
the tricky part is removing a token, since
\mathrm{RsA}
s do not support removing
a data value from a register.
We solve this by using a
lossy remove
: i.e., if
one
token is
to be removed from a place, we simulate it by removing
at least one

token (but potentially more).
This will not preserve
reachability
, but it is enough to preserve

coverability
.
Moreover, there will also be an
initial
part setting the contents of the
registers to reflect the initial marking of
\mathcal{N}
(terminating in
q_{\mathit{main}}
)
and a
final
part that checks the coverability by removing (again in
a lossy way) tokens from places, terminating in a single final state.
∎

Remark 8
.

Since
\mathrm{RsA}
s generalise
\mathrm{NRA}
s, their universality, equivalence, and language
inclusion problems are all undecidable.

4.1
Closure Properties

The closure properties of
\mathrm{RsA}
s are the same as for
\mathrm{NRA}
s.

Theorem 9
.

The following closure properties hold for
\mathrm{RsA}
:

1.

\mathrm{RsA}
is closed under union and intersection.

2.

\mathrm{RsA}
is not closed under complement.

Sketch of proof.

The proofs for closure under union and intersection are standard.
For showing the non-closure under complement, consider the
language
L_{\neg\forall\mathit{repeat}}
from
\cref
ex:lang_negallrep, which can be accepted by

\mathrm{RsA}
.
We use a similar technique as in the proof of Proposition 3.2
in
[
16
]
and show that if there were an
\mathrm{RsA}
accepting its
complement, namely, the language

L_{\forall\mathit{repeat}}=\{w\mid\forall i\exists j\colon i\neq j\land\mathbb{D}[w_{i}]=\mathbb{D}[w_{j}]\},

\mathrm{RsA}
s could be used to decide the emptiness problem of a Minsky machine,
which is a known undecidable problem.
See the full proof in
\cref
sec:proofs_closure_properties for more details.
∎

For
\mathrm{RsA}
s with a limited number of registers, we lose the closure under
intersection.

Theorem 10
.

For each
n\in{\rm Nature}
, the following closure properties hold for
\mathrm{RsA}_{n}
:

1.

\mathrm{RsA}_{n}
is closed under union.

2.

\mathrm{RsA}_{n}
is not closed under intersection and complement.

Sketch of proof.

The proof of closure under union is standard.
For proving non-closure of
\mathrm{RsA}_{1}
under intersection, we consider the two
following
\mathrm{RsA}_{1}
languages

\mathcal{L}^{A}_{1}=\{w\mid\mathbb{D}[w_{1}]=\mathbb{D}[w_{|w|}]\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{L}^{B}_{1}=\{w\mid\mathbb{D}[w_{2}]=\mathbb{D}[w_{|w|-1}]\}

and show that their intersection cannot be accepted by
\mathrm{RsA}_{1}
.
This argument can be extended to
\mathrm{RsA}_{n}
for
n>1
.
Non-closure under complement then follows from De Morgan’s laws.
∎

Theorem 11
.

\mathrm{DRsA}
is closed under union, intersection, and complement.

Proof.

The proofs are standard (product construction and swapping (non)-final states).
∎

Theorem 12
.

For each
n\in{\rm Nature}
, the following closure properties hold for
\mathrm{DRsA}_{n}
:

1.

\mathrm{DRsA}_{n}
is closed under complement.

2.

\mathrm{DRsA}_{n}
is not closed under union and intersection.

Proof.

Proof of closure under complement is standard.
Non-closure under union and intersection is done in the same way as in the proof
of
\cref
thm:rsan-closure.
∎

As with RAs, nondeterminism also allows bigger expressivity for
\mathrm{RsA}
s.

Theorem 13
.

\mathrm{DRsA}\subsetneq\mathrm{RsA}

Proof.

Let us consider the language
L_{\neg\forall\mathit{repeat}}
from the proof of

\cref
thm:rsa-closure, which is expressible using
\mathrm{RsA}
s, and its
complement
L_{\forall\mathit{repeat}}
, which is not expressible using
\mathrm{RsA}
s.
Since
\mathrm{DRsA}
s are closed under complement (
\cref
thm:drsa-closure), if they could
accept
L_{\neg\forall\mathit{repeat}}
, they could also accept
L_{\forall\mathit{repeat}}
, which is
a contradiction.
Therefore,
L_{\neg\forall\mathit{repeat}}\notin\mathrm{DRsA}
.
∎

5
Determinising Register Automata

\mathrm{RsA}
s have the following interesting property: a large class of
\mathrm{NRA}
languages can
be determinised into
\mathrm{DRsA}
s.
In this section, we give a determinisation semi-algorithm and specify
properties of a class of
\mathrm{NRA}
s for which it is complete.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta,I,F)
be an
\mathrm{NRA}
.
We use
\mathbf{R}[q]
for
q\in Q
to denote the set of registers
r
such that
there exists a transition
s\text{\small{\hbox to65.6pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 32.79834pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-23.0078pt}{-0.03874pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{69}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-32.29834pt}{2.48pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{69}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}{}{}}{{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{32.29834pt}{2.48pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{69}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -43.72 3.43 L -35.85 3.43}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.85 3.43 L 42.34 3.43}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{30.59836pt}{2.48pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.11 0 C -0.28 0.28 -1.66 0.83 -3.32 1.8 C -1.66 0.55 -1.66 -0.55 -3.32 -1.8 C -1.66 -0.83 -0.28 -0.28 1.11 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}t\in\Delta
with

(i)

\mathit{up}(r)\neq\bot
and
t=q
or

(ii)

r\in g^{=}\cup g^{\neq}
and
s=q
.

Intuitively,
\mathbf{R}[q]
denotes the set of registers
active
in
q
and given a set of states
S
, we define
\mathbf{R}[S]=\bigcup_{q\in S}\mathbf{R}[q]
.
We call
\mathcal{A}

register-local
if
for all
r\in\mathbf{R}
it holds that if
r\in\mathbf{R}[q]
and
r\in\mathbf{R}[s]

for some states
q,s\in Q
, then
q=s
.
It is easy to see that every
\mathrm{NRA}
can be transformed into the register-local
form by creating a new copy of a register for every state that uses it,
potentially increasing the number of registers to
|Q|\cdot|\mathbf{R}|
.

Furthermore,
we call
\mathcal{A}

single-valued
if there is no reachable
configuration
(q,f)
such that
f(r_{1})=f(r_{2})
for a pair of distinct
registers
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbf{R}
, i.e., there is at most one copy of each data
value in
\mathcal{A}
.
Again, any
\mathrm{NRA}
can be converted into the single-valued form, however, the
number of states can increase to
B_{|\mathbf{R}|}\cdot|Q|
where
B_{n}
is the
n
-th
Bell number.
Intuitively, the transformation is done by creating one copy of
each state for every possible partition of
\mathbf{R}
(the partitions denote which
registers hold the same value), and modifying the transition function
correspondingly.

Input :
Single-valued
\mathrm{NRA}

\mathcal{A}=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta,I,F)

Output :

\mathrm{DRsA}

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime},\mathbf{R},\Delta^{\prime},I^{\prime},F^{\prime})

with
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
or
\bot

1

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\leftarrow\textnormal{{worklist}}\leftarrow I^{\prime}\leftarrow\{(I,c_{0}=\{r\mapsto 0\mid r\in\mathbf{R}\})\}
;

2

;

30

Algorithm 1

Determinisation of an
\mathrm{NRA}
into a
\mathrm{DRsA}

The determinisation (semi-)algorithm for a single-valued
\mathrm{NRA}

\mathcal{A}
is shown
in
\cref
alg:det.
On the high level, it is similar to the classical Rabin-Scott subset
construction for determinising finite automata
[
32
]
with additional
treatment of registers superimposed onto it.
5
5

5

The algorithm can be seen as a simplification of the algorithm for converting
counting automata to deterministic counting-set automata
from
[
47
]
(we do not need to deal with operations on the
values stored in registers), but with additional features necessary to deal with

\mathrm{NRA}
-specific issues.

During the construction, we track

(i)

all states of
\mathcal{A}
in which the runs of
\mathcal{A}
might be at a given
point, represented by a set of states
S\subseteq Q
and

(ii)

the sizes of sets stored in each register,
represented by a mapping
c\colon\mathbf{R}\to\{0,1,\omega\}

(
\omega
denotes any number
\geq 2
)
6
6

6

We keep track of the sizes to detect when our simulation of a disequality
test
\mathit{in}\neq r
by the non-membership test
\mathit{in}\notin r
is imprecise
due to
r
containing two or more elements.

;

the macrostate is then a pair
(S,c)
.
The initial state of the constructed
\mathrm{DRsA}
is the macrostate
(I,c_{0})
where

c_{0}
is a mapping assigning zero to each register (the run of a
\mathrm{DRsA}

starts with all registers initialized to
\emptyset
) (Line
1
).

The main loop of the algorithm then constructs successors of reachable
macrostates for each
a\in\Sigma
and each
g\subseteq\mathbf{R}
on
Line
1
; each pair
a
,
g
corresponds to the so-called

minterm
(minterms denote combinations of guards whose semantics do not
overlap
[
11
]
).
For each minterm, we collect all transitions of
\mathcal{A}
compatible with this
minterm (Line
1
) and generate the successor set of states
S^{\prime}

(Line
1
).
The
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
update function
\mathit{up}^{\prime}
for register
r
is then set to collect
into
r
all possible values that might be stored into
r
in
\mathcal{A}
on any run
over the input word at the given position
(Lines
1
–
1
).

The algorithm needs to avoid the following possible issues:

1.

Since the algorithm collects in the set-register
r
all possible values
that could have been stored into the standard register
r
in
\mathcal{A}
, if the
disequality tests in
g^{\neq}
were changed for non-membership tests
in
g^{\notin}
, this could mean that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
might not be able to simulate
some transition of
\mathcal{A}
(the transition would not be enabled).
Consider the following example:

q
s
t
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{q}\leftarrow\mathit{in}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{q}\leftarrow r_{q}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\neq r_{q}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{s}\leftarrow r_{q}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}=r_{s}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}

(a) An
\mathrm{NRA}

\mathcal{A}
with a disequality guard

\{q\}
\{q,s\}
\cdots
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\in r_{q}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{q}\leftarrow r_{q}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\notin r_{q}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{q}\leftarrow r_{q}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}\\
r_{s}\leftarrow r_{q}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\notin r_{q}\\
\mathit{in}\notin r_{s}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{q}\leftarrow r_{q}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}\\
r_{s}\leftarrow r_{q}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\in r_{q}\\
\mathit{in}\notin r_{s}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{q}\leftarrow r_{q}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}\end{array}\end{array}

(b) A part of the
\mathrm{RsA}
obtained for
\mathcal{A}

where (b) contains a part of the
\mathrm{RsA}
obtained if
\cref
alg:det did not
use the
c
-component of macrostates.
Notice that while
\mathcal{A}
accepts the word

\langle a,1\rangle\langle a,2\rangle\langle a,2\rangle\langle a,1\rangle
, the
\mathrm{RsA}
obtained in this way
does not.
The reason for this is that after reading the third symbol (i.e.,
\langle a,2\rangle
), the
\mathrm{RsA}
goes to the macrostate
\{q\}
—it thinks it cannot be
in
s
any more.

This is the reason why we augment macrostates with the
c
-component.
If we detect that a disequality test is performed on a register
containing more than one element, we terminate the algorithm
(Line
1
).
The tracking of sizes of sets stored in registers is done on
Line
1

where the sum is
saturated
to
\omega
for values
{}>1
.

2.

By collecting all possible values that can occur in registers, the
algorithm is performing the so-called
Cartesian abstraction
(i.e., it
is losing information about dependencies between components in tuples).
This can lead to a scenario where, for some set-register assignment
f^{\prime}

of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, we would have
d_{1}\in f^{\prime}(r_{1})
and
d_{2}\in f^{\prime}(r_{2})
, but
there would be no corresponding configuration of
\mathcal{A}
with register
assignment
f
such that
d_{1}=f(r_{1})
and
d_{2}=f(r_{2})
.
Consider, e.g., an
\mathrm{NRA}
for the language

\{uvwvz\mid u,w,z\in(\Sigma\times\mathbb{D})^{*},|v|=2\}
:

q
s
t
u
v
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{1}\leftarrow\mathit{in}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{2}\leftarrow r_{1}\\
r_{3}\leftarrow\mathit{in}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{2}\leftarrow r_{2}\\
r_{3}\leftarrow r_{3}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}=r_{2}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{4}\leftarrow r_{3}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}=r_{4}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}

When the algorithm computes the successor of the macrostate
(\{q,s,t\},\{r_{1}{:}1,r_{2}{:}1,r_{3}{:}1\})
over
a\in\Sigma
and the guard
g=\emptyset
, it would obtain the following update of registers:

r_{1}\leftarrow\{\mathit{in}\}
(transition from
q
to
s
),

r_{2}\leftarrow r_{1}\cup r_{2}
(transition from
s
to
t
and transition from
t

to
t
), and

r_{3}\leftarrow r_{3}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}
(transition from
s
to
t
and transition
from
t
to
t
).
This would simulate the update
r_{2}\leftarrow r_{2}
,
r_{3}\leftarrow\mathit{in}
, which is
nowhere in the original
\mathrm{NRA}
.
The algorithm detects the possibility of such an overapproximation on
Lines
1
–
1
.

3.

We need to avoid a situation when a set-register has collected all
possible nondeterministic choices of a standard
\mathrm{NRA}
register and then is
tested twice with a different result.
Consider the following example of an
\mathrm{NRA}

\mathcal{A}
and an
\mathrm{RsA}
obtained
from
\mathcal{A}
by
\cref
alg:det without Line
1
(to
save space, we collapse all macrostates with the same set of states into one):

q
s
t
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{q}\leftarrow\mathit{in}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{q}\leftarrow r_{q}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$b$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}=r_{q}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{s}\leftarrow r_{q}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$b$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}=r_{s}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}

(a) An
\mathrm{NRA}

\mathcal{A}

\{q\}
\{s\}
\{t\}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{q}\leftarrow r_{q}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$b$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\in r_{q}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{s}\leftarrow r_{q}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$b$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\in r_{s}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}

(b) An
\mathrm{RsA}
overapproximating
\mathcal{A}
’s language

One can see that while the
\mathrm{NRA}
cannot accept the word

\langle a,1\rangle\langle a,2\rangle\langle b,1\rangle\langle b,2\rangle
, the
\mathrm{RsA}
accepts it.
This happens because the
\mathrm{RsA}
did not “
collapse
” the possible
nondeterministic choices that are kept in the registers for the value
of
r_{q}
after the first membership test (on the transition from
\{q\}

to
\{s\}
) succeeded.
We avoid this situation by the code on Line
1
,
which performs the collapse of the set of nondeterministic choices into
a single value when it is positively tested.
The update on the
\mathrm{RsA}
transition from
\{q\}
to
\{s\}

constructed by the algorithm will then become
r_{s}\leftarrow\{\mathit{in}\}
and the
result will be precise.

One might also imagine similar scenario as the previous but with several
registers copying a nondeterministically chosen value (e.g., when
a data value is copied from
r_{1}
to
r_{2}
and, later,
r_{1}
is
positively tested for equality, we need to guarantee that the value of
r_{2}

also collapses to the given data value).
In order to avoid this, we require that the input
\mathrm{NRA}
is

single-valued
, i.e., it never happens that a data value is in more
than one register.

Theorem 14
.

When
\cref
alg:det returns a
\mathrm{DRsA}

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.

Naturally, we wish to syntactically characterise the class of
\mathrm{NRA}
s for which

\cref
alg:det is complete.
We observe that when we start with an
\mathrm{NRA}^{=}_{1}
and transform it into
the single-valued register-local form, the algorithm always returns a
\mathrm{DRsA}
.

Theorem 15
.

(a)

For every
\mathrm{NRA}^{=}_{1}
, there exists a
\mathrm{DRsA}
accepting the same language.

(b)

For every
\mathrm{URA}^{=}_{1}
, there exists a
\mathrm{DRsA}
accepting the same language.

Let
\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{NRA}^{=}_{1})
be the class of languages that can be expressed using
a Boolean combination of
\mathrm{NRA}^{=}_{1}
languages, i.e., it is the closure of

\mathrm{NRA}^{=}_{1}
languages under union, and intersection, and complement (it could
also be denoted as
\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{URA}^{=}_{1})
).

Example 16
.

For instance, the language
L_{\exists,\neg\exists\mathit{repeat}}
composed as the concatenation of

L_{\exists\mathit{repeat}}
and
L_{\neg\exists\mathit{repeat}}
with a delimiter, formally

L_{\exists,\neg\exists\mathit{repeat}}=L_{\exists\mathit{repeat}}\mathbin{\cdot}\{\langle b,d\rangle\mid d\in\mathbb{D}\}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{\neg\exists\mathit{repeat}},

is in
\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{NRA}^{=}_{1})
, since it is the intersection of languages

L_{\exists\mathit{repeat}}\mathbin{\cdot}\{\langle b,d\rangle\mid d\in\mathbb{D}\}\mathbin{\cdot}\{\langle a,d\rangle\mid d\in\mathbb{D}\}^{*}~\text{and}~\{\langle a,d\rangle\mid d\in\mathbb{D}\}^{*}\mathbin{\cdot}\{\langle b,d\rangle\mid d\in\mathbb{D}\}\mathbin{\cdot}L_{\neg\exists\mathit{repeat}},

but is expressible neither by an
\mathrm{NRA}
nor by a
\mathrm{URA}
(
\mathrm{URA}
s cannot express
the part
before
the delimiter and
\mathrm{NRA}
s cannot express the part

after
the delimiter).
∎

From
\cref
thm:nra1-to-drsa we can conclude that

\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{NRA}^{=}_{1})
is captured by
\mathrm{DRsA}
.

Corollary 17
.

For any language in
\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{NRA}^{=}_{1})
, there exists a
\mathrm{DRsA}
accepting it.

Sketch of proof.

Follows from
\cref
thm:nra1-to-drsa,thm:drsa-closure.
∎

Corollary 18
.

The inclusion problem between
\mathrm{RsA}
and
\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{NRA}^{=}_{1})
is decidable.

Proof.

We just write
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
as
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\cap\overline{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{2})\vphantom{t}}=\emptyset
and use

\cref
thm:boolean-drsa,thm:rsa-closure,thm:rsa-emptiness.
∎

6
Expressivity of Register Set Automata

In this section, we position
\mathrm{RsA}
s in the landscape of automata over data
words.
For this, we use the languages introduced previously and some other languages
defined below; these languages are then used to distinguish various register
automata models (due to space constraints, this listing is by no means
exhaustive).

Example 19
.

First, we define the language over
\Sigma=\{a,b\}

L_{\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}=\{w\mid\exists i\colon a[w_{i}]\land\nexists j<i\colon b[w_{j}]\land\mathbb{D}[w_{j}]=\mathbb{D}[w_{i}]\}

from
[
16
, Proof of Proposition 3.2]

and its complement
L_{\neg\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}
.
Intuitively,
L_{\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}
is the language of words
w
such that there exists
an input element
\langle a,d\rangle
that is not preceded by an occurrence of a
\langle b,d\rangle
element.
Neither
L_{\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}
nor
L_{\neg\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}
can be accepted by
\mathrm{ARA}_{1}
while

\mathrm{ARA}_{1}(\mathsf{g,s})
accepts
L_{\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}
but cannot accept

L_{\neg\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}
.
On the other hand, as shown
in
\cref
fig:drsa_langexanob,fig:drsa_langnegexanob,
\mathrm{DRsA}_{1}
can accept
both
L_{\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}
and
L_{\neg\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}
.

(It might seem suspicious that
\mathrm{DRsA}_{1}
can express the
language
L_{\neg\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}
, since according to
[
16
, Proof of
Proposition 3.2]
, if an
\mathrm{ARA}_{1}

\mathcal{A}
could accept the language,

\mathcal{A}
could be used to decide language-emptiness of a Minsky machine.
So how come that we can express
L_{\neg\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}
using
\mathrm{DRsA}_{1}
, which have
a decidable emptiness problem (cf.
\cref
thm:rsa-emptiness)?
The reason is that in the construction of the automaton representing the accepting
runs of a Minsky machine from
[
16
]
, apart from
L_{\neg\exists\mathit{a\text{-}no\text{-}b}}
,
we also need to be able to express the property “
every counter
increment is matched with its decrement
”, which is not expressible by
\mathrm{RsA}

(cf. the proof of
\cref
thm:rsa-closure).)
∎

Example 20
.

Moreover, let us define the following language
from
[
12
, Example 2.2]
:

L_{\forall a\exists b}=\big\{w\mid\forall i\colon a[w_{i}]\implies\big((\forall j\neq i\colon a[w_{j}]\implies\mathbb{D}[w_{i}]\neq\mathbb{D}[w_{j}])\land{}

\hskip 128.60645pt(\exists k>i\colon b[w_{k}]\land\mathbb{D}[w_{i}]=\mathbb{D}[w_{k}])\big)\big\}\hskip 15.50673pt

Intuitively,
L_{\forall a\exists b}
denotes the language of words where no two

a
-positions contain the same data value and every
a
-position is followed by
a matching
b
-position.

L_{\forall a\exists b}
is recognizable by
\mathrm{ARA}_{1}

[
12
, Example 2.6]
, but
is not recognizable by any
\mathrm{URA}
,
\mathrm{NRA}
, or
\mathrm{RsA}
.
∎

The Hasse diagram comparing the expressive power of a selection of register
automata models with decidable emptiness problem is in

\cref
fig:expressive_power and languages that distinguish the various classes
are in
\cref
tab:languages.

Remark 21
.

\mathrm{RsA}
s are also incomparable to the class of
pebble
automata

[
31
]
, since
\mathrm{DRsA}
s generalize
\mathrm{DRA}
s, they can accept
a language not expressible by
\mathrm{PA}
s (as shown by Tan in
[
43
]
).
On the other hand,
\mathrm{RsA}
s cannot express
L_{\forall\mathit{repeat}}
, which is expressible
by
\mathrm{PA}
s.
∎

\mathrm{RsA}
model.

results, see
\cref
sec:app_extensions for more details.

registers in
g^{=\emptyset}
are empty.
It is easy to see that each such
\mathrm{RsA}^{=\emptyset}
can be transformed into
a standard
\mathrm{RsA}
with
2^{|\mathbf{R}|}\cdot|Q|
states by keeping
track of which registers are empty and which are not, in each state.

This extension could be generalized to an extension where on each transition,
we could ask, for every register
r\in\mathbf{R}
, whether the number of elements
in the register is equal to
k
for
k<n
, where
n\in{\rm Nature}_{0}
is an

a priori
fixed number.
The translation to an
\mathrm{RsA}
with
(n+1)^{|\mathbf{R}|}\cdot|Q|
states can be done
in a similar way as for
\mathrm{RsA}^{=\emptyset}
.

RsAs with Register Equality Test.

The second extension
\mathrm{RsA}^{=r}
enables to test
equality
of registers.

is enabled if, in addition to the standard semantics, the equality of the
content of registers specified by
g^{=}
also holds.
Unfortunately, this model is too powerful: already its emptiness problem is
undecidable.
The undecidability can be proved by a reduction from the reachability
problem of Petri nets with inhibitor arcs, which is undecidable.

RsAs with Removal.

The next extension
\mathrm{RsA}^{\mathit{rm}}
allows the update operation to also

remove
from registers the current data value on the input.

\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-completeness of emptiness testing can be proved in a similar way as
for
\cref
thm:rsa-emptiness.

RsAs with Removals and Register Emptiness Test.

The next extension
\mathrm{RsA}^{\mathit{rm}}_{=\emptyset}
allows the update operation to

remove
from registers the current data value and also test
emptiness.
Although this might seem like an innocent combination of the models
\mathrm{RsA}^{=\emptyset}

and
\mathrm{RsA}^{\mathit{rm}}
with decidable emptiness, the problem is
undecidable
for
the new model.
Similarly as for
\mathrm{RsA}^{=r}
, the undecidability can be shown by
a reduction from reachability in Petri nets with inhibitor arcs.

8
Related Work

The literature on automata over infinite alphabets is rich, see, e.g., the
excellent survey by Segoufin
[
42
]
and the paper by Neven
et
al.

[
31
]
.

Register automata were introduced (under the name
finite memory
automata
) by Kaminski and Francez in
[
26
]
and their basic
properties further studied by Sakamoto and Ikeda in
[
36
]
.
Demri and Lazić study in
[
12
]

(non-)deterministic, universal, and alternating one-way and two-way register
automata, and their relation to the linear temporal logic with the
freeze

quantifier, which can store the current data value into a register.
In particular, they show that
\mathrm{LTL}^{\downarrow}_{1}(\mathtt{X},\mathtt{U})
, i.e., the
fragment of the logic with one freeze register and the
next
(
\mathtt{X}
)
and
until
(
\mathtt{U}
) temporal operators captures the class of
languages accepted by one-way alternating register automata with one register
(
\mathrm{ARA}_{1}
).

Figueira
[
16
]
introduces alternating register automata with one
register and two extra operations:
\mathsf{guess}
and
\mathsf{spread}
(
\mathrm{ARA}_{1}(\mathsf{g,s})
).
Intuitively,
\mathsf{guess}
can guess an arbitrary data value and store it in
the register (which can be used to encode an existential quantification over future data
values) and
\mathsf{spread}
orchestrates all threads whose register value matches
the current data value on the input tape to make a transition into a new state
(which can be used to encode a sort of universal quantification over past data values).

Symbolic register automata
[
13
]
extend the register automaton
model with the ability to test predicates over the data values (which need to
come from some decidable first-order theory) while preserving most of the theoretical
properties of register automata.

Figueira
et al.

[
17
]
noticed the tight connection between
register and timed automata, allowing to transfer results between the
models.
Clemente
et al.

[
9
,
10
]
consider the problem of

determinisability
of register/timed automata, i.e., whether
a nondeterministic register/timed automaton can be transformed into
a deterministic register/timed automaton with the same language.
In this paper, we use a more general model (
\mathrm{RsA}
s) for the
determinisation.

Bojańczyk
et al.

[
4
]
introduce a model of

nominal automata
, register automata where not only equality, but also
total and partial order tests between symbols are allowed.
On the other hand,
Chen
et al.

[
8
,
7
]
extend register automata with the
capability to compute affine functions over rationals and show decidability of
reachability and the commutativity problem for fragments of the model.

In our work, we were inspired by
counting-set automata
introduced by Turoňová
et
al.

[
47
]
, which use
sets of counter values
to compactly
represent configurations of
counting automata

[
22
]
(a restricted
version of counter automata
[
27
]
with a bound on the value of
counters for compact representation of finite automata), to obtain
a deterministic model for efficient matching of regular expressions with
repetitions.

Pebble automata (PAs)
[
46
]
are a model for automata over infinite words based on
a different principle than RAs: a PA can
mark
certain
positions in the input word using
pebbles
and make decisions based on
equality of data values under the automaton head and the pebbles.
The class of PAs is quite robust—they are closed under Boolean operations,
determinisable, and the two-way and one-way versions have the same expressive
power.
On the other hand, PAs are not really practical—language emptiness is already
undecidable.

There have been several attempts of automata models for matching regexes with
back-references, but they have generally failed expectations of the community.
Memory automata of Schmid
[
37
]
provide a natural extension of finite
automata with a register bank, each register able to store a word, but do not
allow determinisation.
Becchi and Crowley
[
3
]
encode back-references into an extended
model of NFAs using backtracking.
Namjoshi and Narlikar
[
29
]
extend Thompson’s
algorithm
[
45
]
to a model similar to the models
of
[
37
,
3
]
in the obvious way by tracking the set of all
possible configurations, which is inefficient (for each symbol, the number of
operations proportional to the (potentially exponential) size of the set of
configurations is required).
Moreover, there are also some proprietary ad hoc industrial solutions that support fast
matching of regexes with back-references
[
44
]
.

9
Conclusion and Future Work

We have introduced register set automata, a class of automata over data words
providing an underlying formal model for efficient pattern matching of
a subclass of regexes with back-references.
There are many challenges that we wish to address in the future:

(i)

improvement of our determinisation algorithm to work on a larger
class of input
\mathrm{NRA}
s,

(ii)

explore other, more expressive, formal models that would
allow deterministic automata models for a large class of regexes with
backreferences (e.g., the hard regex in
footnote
2
is beyond the power of
\mathrm{DRsA}
s),

(iii)

develop efficient toolbox for working with
\mathrm{RsA}
s occurring in
practice.

A.1
Proof of
\cref
thm:rsa-emptiness

See
7

The proof is done by showing interreducibility of
\mathrm{RsA}
emptiness with
coverability in
transfer Petri nets
(often used for modelling the
so-called
broadcast protocols
), which is a known
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-complete
problem
[
41
,
39
,
40
]
.
We start with defining transfer Petri nets and then give the reductions.

A.1.1
Transfer Petri Nets

Intuitively, transfer Petri net is an extension of Petri nets where transitions can

transfer
all tokens from one place to another place at once.
They are closely related to
broadcast protocols

[
14
]
.

Formally,
a
transfer Petri net
(TPN) is a triple
\mathcal{N}=(P,T,M_{0}),
s.t.
P
is a finite set of
places
,

T
is a finite set of
transitions
, and

M_{0}\colon P\to{\rm Nature}
is an
initial marking
.
The set of transitions
T
is such that
P\cap T=\emptyset
and every transition
t\in T
is of the form

t=\langle\mathit{In},\mathit{Out},\mathit{Transfer}\rangle
where
\mathit{In},\mathit{Out}\colon P\to{\rm Nature}
define
t
’s
input
and
output places
respectively and

\mathit{Transfer}\colon P\to P
is a (total)
transfer function
.

A
marking
of
\mathcal{N}
is a function
M\colon P\to{\rm Nature}
assigning
a particular number of tokens to each state.
Given a pair of markings
M
and
M^{\prime}
, we use
M\leq M^{\prime}
to denote that
for all
p\in P
it holds that
M(p)\leq M^{\prime}(p)
.
Moreover, we use
M+M^{\prime}
and
M-M^{\prime}
(for
M^{\prime}\leq M
) to denote the
pointwise addition and subtraction of markings and we use
\mathbf{u}_{p}
to denote
the marking such that
\mathbf{u}_{p}(p^{\prime})=1
if
p=p^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{u}_{p}(p^{\prime})=0

otherwise, and
\vec{0}
to denote the marking
\{p\mapsto 0\mid p\in P\}
.
The
identity
function (over an arbitrary set that is clear from the
context) is denoted as
\mathbf{id}
.
Given a marking
M
, a transition
t=\langle\mathit{In},\mathit{Out},\mathit{Transfer}\rangle
is

enabled
if
\mathit{In}\leq M
,
i.e., there is a sufficient number of tokens in each of its input places.
We use
M[t\rangle M^{\prime}
to denote that

1.

t
is enabled in
M
and

2.

M^{\prime}
is the marking such that for every
p\in P
we have

M^{\prime}(p)=\mathit{Out}(p)+\sum\{M_{\mathit{aux}}(p^{\prime})\mid\mathit{Transfer}(p^{\prime})=p\}\text{ where }M_{\mathit{aux}}=M-\mathit{In}.

(1)

That is, the successor marking
M^{\prime}
is obtained by

(i)

removing
\mathit{In}
tokens from inputs of
t
,

(ii)

transferring tokens according to
\mathit{Transfer}
, and

(iii)

adding
\mathit{Out}
tokens to
t
’s outputs.

We say that a marking
M
is
reachable
if there is a (possibly empty)
sequence
t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{n}
of transitions such that it holds that

M_{0}[t_{1}\rangle M_{1}[t_{2}\rangle\ldots[t_{n}\rangle M
, where
M_{0}
is the initial
marking.
A marking
M
is
coverable
, if there exists a reachable marking
M^{\prime}
,
such that
M\leq M^{\prime}
.
The
Coverability
problem for TPNs asks, given a TPN
\mathcal{N}
and
a marking
M
, whether
M
is coverable in
\mathcal{N}
.

Proposition 22

(
[
41
]
)
.

The
Coverability
problem for TPNs is
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-complete.

Let us now prove the two directions of the proof of
\cref
thm:rsa-emptiness.

Lemma 23
.

The emptiness problem for
\mathrm{RsA}
is in
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
.

Proof.

The proof of the lemma is based on reducing the
\mathrm{RsA}
emptiness problem to
coverability in TPNs, which is
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-complete
(
\cref
prop:tpn-coverability-fomega-complete).
Intuitively, the reduction consists of creating a TPN with
places representing both individual states of
\mathrm{RsA}
and
individual
regions
of the Venn diagram of
\mathbf{R}
.
Transitions of
\mathrm{RsA}
are represented by one or more transitions
of TPN, distinguishing every possible option of
\mathit{in}
being
in some region
\mathit{rgn}\in 2^{\mathbf{R}}
.
The set of arcs leading to and from each transition is calculated in a way which preserves the semantics and position of values defined by the
guard
and
update
formulae.
Finally, the marking to be covered requires one token
to be present in the places representing the final states of the
\mathrm{RsA}
.

Formally, let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta,I,F)
be an
\mathrm{RsA}
.
In the following, we will construct a TPN
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}=(P,T,M_{0})
and
a marking
M_{F}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\neq\emptyset
iff
M_{F}
is coverable
in
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
We set the components of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}
as follows:

•

P=Q\uplus\{\mathit{init},\mathit{fin}\}\uplus 2^{\mathbf{R}}
where

\mathit{init}
and
\mathit{fin}
are two new places,

•

T=\{\langle\mathbf{u}_{\mathit{init}},\mathbf{u}_{q_{i}},\mathbf{id}\rangle\mid q_{i}\in I\}\cup\{\langle\mathbf{u}_{q_{f}},\mathbf{u}_{\mathit{fin}},\mathbf{id}\rangle\mid q_{f}\in F\}\cup T^{\prime}
with
T^{\prime}
defined below, and

•

M_{0}=\mathbf{u}_{\mathit{init}}
.

Intuitively, the set of places contains the states of
\mathcal{A}
(there will always
be at most one token in those places), two new places
\mathit{init}
and

\mathit{fin}
that are used for the initial nondeterministic choice of some
initial state of
\mathcal{A}
and for a unique
final place
(whose coverability
will be checked) respectively, and, finally, a new place for every

region
of the Venn diagram of
\mathbf{R}
, which will track the number of
data values that two or more registers share (e.g., for
\mathbf{R}=\{r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\}
, the subset
\{r_{1},r_{3}\}
denotes the region
r_{1}\cap\overline{r_{2}\vphantom{t}}\cap r_{3}
, i.e., the data values that are stored in
r_{1}

and
r_{3}
but are not stored in
r_{2}
. The region
\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap\overline{r_{2}\vphantom{t}}\cap\overline{r_{3}\vphantom{t}}
is denoted as
\{\emptyset\}
.
Regions
\mathit{rgn}_{1},\mathit{rgn}_{2}
are
distinct
,
if
\mathit{rgn}_{1}\triangle\mathit{rgn}_{2}\neq\emptyset
, i.e.

\exists r\colon(r\in\mathit{rgn}_{1}\lor r\in\mathit{rgn}_{2})\land r\notin\mathit{rgn}_{1}\cap\mathit{rgn}_{2}
.
The set of TPN transitions
T^{\prime}
is defined below.

We now proceed to the definition of
T^{\prime}
.
Let
t=q\text{\small{\hbox to69.34pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.67073pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.94 12.28 L -31.94 12.28 C -35.37 12.28 -38.16 9.49 -38.16 6.05 L -38.16 0.81 C -38.16 -2.63 -35.37 -5.42 -31.94 -5.42 L 31.94 -5.42 C 35.37 -5.42 38.16 -2.63 38.16 0.81 L 38.16 6.05 C 38.16 9.49 35.37 12.28 31.94 12.28 Z M -38.16 -5.42}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-24.88019pt}{-0.03874pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{69}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}

be
a transition in
\mathcal{A}
.
Then, we create a TPN transition for every possible option of
\mathit{in}

being in some region
\mathit{rgn}_{g}\in 2^{\mathbf{R}}
(e.g., for
\mathit{in}\in r_{1}\cap\overline{r_{2}\vphantom{t}}\cap r_{3}
or
\mathit{in}\in\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap\overline{r_{2}\vphantom{t}}\cap r_{3}
).
For
t
and
\mathit{rgn}_{g}
, we define

\gamma(t,\mathit{rgn}_{g})=\begin{cases}\{\langle\mathit{In},\mathit{Out},\mathit{Transfer}\rangle\}&\text{if }(g^{\in}\subseteq\mathit{rgn}_{g})\land(g^{\notin}\cap\mathit{rgn}_{g}=\emptyset)\text{ and}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2)

Then
T^{\prime}=\bigcup\{\gamma(t,\mathit{rgn}_{g})\mid t\in\Delta,\mathit{rgn}_{g}\in 2^{\mathbf{R}}\}\cup\{\langle\vec{0},\mathbf{u}_{\emptyset},\mathbf{id}\rangle\}
.
Note that the task of the TPN transition
\langle\vec{0},\mathbf{u}_{\emptyset},\mathbf{id}\rangle
is to provide an arbitrary number of tokens in the place for the
region

\{\emptyset\}=\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap\ldots\cap\overline{r_{n}\vphantom{t}}
, which corresponds to an unlimited
number of input data values.

In the definition of
\gamma(t,\mathit{rgn}_{g})
above,
\mathit{In}
,
\mathit{Out}
, and

\mathit{Transfer}
are defined as follows:

•

\mathit{In}=\mathbf{u}_{\mathit{rgn}_{g}}+\mathbf{u}_{q}
and

•

\mathit{Out}=\mathbf{u}_{\mathit{dst}}+\mathbf{u}_{s}
where

\mathit{dst}=\{r_{i}\in\mathbf{R}\mid(\{\mathit{in}\}\cup\mathit{rgn}_{g})\cap\mathit{up}(r_{i})\neq\emptyset\}
.

with

\mathit{up}(r_{1})=\{r_{1},\mathit{in}\}
and
\mathit{up}(r_{2})=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
.
We need to update the following four regions of the Venn diagram of
r_{1}

and
r_{2}
:
r_{1}\cap r_{2}
,
r_{1}\cap\overline{r_{2}\vphantom{t}}
,
\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap r_{2}
, and
\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap\overline{r_{2}\vphantom{t}}
.
From the update function
\mathit{up}
, we see that the new values stored
in
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
will be (we used primed versions of register names to
denote their value after update)
r_{1}^{\prime}=r_{1}
(we do not consider

\{\mathit{in}\}
here because it has been discharged within
\mathit{Out}
in the
previous step) and
r_{2}^{\prime}=r_{1}\cup r_{2}
.
The values of the regions will therefore be updated as follows:

\displaystyle r_{1}^{\prime}\cap r_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=r_{1}\cap(r_{1}\cup r_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptr_{1}^{\prime}\cap\overline{r_{2}^{\prime}\vphantom{t}}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=r_{1}\cap\overline{(r_{1}\cup r_{2})\vphantom{t}}

\displaystyle\overline{r_{1}^{\prime}\vphantom{t}}\cap r_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap(r_{1}\cup r_{2})

\displaystyle\overline{r_{1}^{\prime}\vphantom{t}}\cap\overline{r_{2}^{\prime}\vphantom{t}}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap\overline{(r_{1}\cup r_{2})\vphantom{t}}\hskip-14.22636pt

(3)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=(r_{1}\cap r_{1})\cup(r_{1}\cap r_{2})

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=r_{1}\cap\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap\overline{r_{2}\vphantom{t}}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=(\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap r_{1})\cup(\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap r_{2})\hskip-14.22636pt

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap\overline{r_{2}\vphantom{t}}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=r_{1}\cup(r_{1}\cap r_{2})

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap r_{2}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=\overline{r_{1}\vphantom{t}}\cap\overline{r_{2}\vphantom{t}}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle=(r_{1}\cap\overline{r_{2}\vphantom{t}})\cup(r_{1}\cap r_{2})

Note that in the last step of the calculation of
r_{1}^{\prime}\cap r_{2}^{\prime}
, we used
the fact that
r_{1}=(r_{1}\cap r_{2})\cup(r_{1}\cap\overline{r_{2}\vphantom{t}})
in order
to obtain a union of regions.
From the previous calculation, we see that
\mathit{Transfer}
should be set as
follows:

\mathit{Transfer}(\{r_{1},r_{2}\})=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
,

\mathit{Transfer}(\{r_{1}\})=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
,

\mathit{Transfer}(\{r_{2}\})=\{r_{2}\}
, and

\mathit{Transfer}(\{\emptyset\})=\{\emptyset\}
.

Figure 7:
An example
\mathrm{RsA}

Formally,
\mathit{Transfer}
is computed as follows.
For every
\mathit{rgn}_{o}\in 2^{\mathbf{R}}
, let us compute the sets of sets of registers

\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn}_{o})=\{\mathit{up}(r_{i})\cap\mathbf{R}\mid r_{i}\in\mathit{rgn}_{o}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn}_{o})=\bigcup\{\mathit{up}(r_{i})\cap\mathbf{R}\mid r_{i}\notin\mathit{rgn}_{o}\}

(4)

(
\mathit{pst}
is for “
positive
” and
\mathit{ngt}
is for
“
negative
”, which represent registers that occur positively and
negatively, respectively, in the specification of the region of the Venn
diagram
\mathit{rgn}_{o}
).
The intuition is that
\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn}_{o})
represents the update
of
\mathit{rgn}_{o}
as the
product of sums
(intersection of unions),
cf. the first line in
\cref
eq:rsa_emptiness_example_transfer in

\cref
ex:rsa_emptiness_example_transfer.
Next, we convert the product of sums
\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn}_{o})
into
a sum of products

\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}(\mathit{rgn}_{o})=\coprod\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn}_{o})

(5)

where
\coprod\{D_{1},\dots,D_{n}\}
is the
unordered Cartesian
product
of sets
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}
, i.e.,

\coprod\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\}=\bigl\{\{d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\}\bigm|(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\in D_{1}\times\cdots\times D_{n}\bigr\}.

(6)

Example 25
.

In the transition considered in
\cref
ex:rsa_emptiness_example_transfer, we
obtain the following:

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\{r_{1},r_{2}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\{r_{1}\},\{r_{1},r_{2}\}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\{r_{1}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\{r_{1}\}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\{r_{2}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\{r_{1},r_{2}\}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\{\emptyset\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\{\emptyset\}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}(\{r_{1},r_{2}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\{r_{1}\},\{r_{1},r_{2}\}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}(\{r_{1}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\{r_{1}\}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}(\{r_{2}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\{r_{1}\},\{r_{2}\}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}(\{\emptyset\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\{\emptyset\}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{ngt}(\{r_{1},r_{2}\})

\displaystyle{}=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathit{ngt}(\{r_{1}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{ngt}(\{r_{2}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{r_{1}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{ngt}(\{\emptyset\})

\displaystyle{}=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}~\hfill\vartriangleleft

Next, we modify
\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}
into
\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
by removing regions that are
incompatible with
\mathit{ngt}
to obtain

\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\mathit{rgn}_{o})=\{x\in\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}(\mathit{rgn}_{o})\mid x\cap\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn}_{o})=\emptyset\}.

(7)

Example 26
.

In the running example, we would obtain the following values of
\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\{r_{1},r_{2}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\{r_{1}\},\{r_{1},r_{2}\}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\{r_{1}\})

\displaystyle{}=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\{r_{2}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\{r_{2}\}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\{\emptyset\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\{\emptyset\}\}

Compare the results with the calculation in
\cref
eq:rsa_emptiness_example_transfer.

\vartriangleleft

Lastly, for every
\mathit{rgn}_{i}\in 2^{\mathbf{R}}
such that
\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\mathit{rgn}_{o})\in\mathit{rgn}_{i}
, we set
\mathit{Transfer}(\mathit{rgn}_{i})=\mathit{rgn}_{o}
.

Example 27
.

Continuing in the running example, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathit{Transfer}(\{r_{1},r_{2}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{Transfer}(\{r_{1}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{Transfer}(\{r_{2}\})

\displaystyle{}=\{r_{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathit{Transfer}(\{\emptyset\})

\displaystyle{}=\{\emptyset\},

which is the same result as in
\cref
ex:rsa_emptiness_example_transfer.

\cref
fig:tpn_example contains the TPN fragment for all TPN transitions
constructed from
\mathcal{A}
’s transition
