.

\vartriangleleft

The following claim shows that this construction is indeed well defined.

Claim 28
.

The function
\mathit{Transfer}
is well defined.

{claimproof}

It is necessary to show that no set of values will be
duplicated and assigned to two distinct regions when
\mathit{Transfer}
is calculated.
According to the definition of
\mathit{Transfer}
,
for all regions
\mathit{rgn}^{\prime}\in 2^{\mathbf{R}}
such that

\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\mathit{rgn}_{o})\in\mathit{rgn}^{\prime}
, the value of
\mathit{Transfer}(\mathit{rgn}^{\prime})
is set to be

\mathit{rgn}_{o}
, therefore we need to prove that for each pair of distinct
regions
\mathit{rgn}_{1}
and
\mathit{rgn}_{2}\in 2^{\mathbf{R}}
it holds that

\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\mathit{rgn}_{1})\cap\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\mathit{rgn}_{2})=\emptyset
.
We prove this by contradiction.

Assume that there are two
distinct regions
\mathit{rgn}_{1}
and
\mathit{rgn}_{2}
such that
there exists a region
\mathit{rgn}_{3}\in\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\mathit{rgn}_{1})\cap\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\mathit{rgn}_{2})
.
According to the construction of
\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathit{rgn}_{3}\in\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}(\mathit{rgn}_{1})\quad\land\quad

\displaystyle\mathit{rgn}_{3}\cap\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn}_{1})=\emptyset\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathit{rgn}_{3}\in\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}(\mathit{rgn}_{2})\quad\land\quad

\displaystyle\mathit{rgn}_{3}\cap\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn}_{2})=\emptyset.

Then, according to the construction of
\mathit{pst}_{\Sigma}
,

\displaystyle\mathit{rgn}_{3}\in\coprod\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn}_{1})\quad\land\quad

\displaystyle\mathit{rgn}_{3}\cap\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn}_{1})=\emptyset\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathit{rgn}_{3}\in\coprod\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn}_{2})\quad\land\quad

\displaystyle\mathit{rgn}_{3}\cap\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn}_{2})=\emptyset.

Following the construction of
\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}
:

\displaystyle(\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\exists P\in\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn}_{1})\colon r\in P)\land(\forall P^{\prime}\in\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn}_{1})\exists r^{\prime}\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\colon r^{\prime}\in P^{\prime})\land(\mathit{rgn}_{3}\cap\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn}_{1})=\emptyset)\land{}

\displaystyle(\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\exists P\in\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn}_{2})\colon r\in P)\land(\forall P^{\prime}\in\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn}_{2})\exists r^{\prime}\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\colon r^{\prime}\in P^{\prime})\land(\mathit{rgn}_{3}\cap\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn}_{2})=\emptyset).

According to the construction of
\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}
, it holds that
if
P\in\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn})
then there exists a register
r\in\mathit{rgn}

such that
P=\mathit{up}(r_{i})
.
Therefore, for each
P\in\mathit{pst}_{\Pi}(\mathit{rgn})
there exists a register
r^{\prime}\in\mathit{rgn}^{\prime}
such that
r\in P
.
Then, continuing in the proof, we obtain

\displaystyle(\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\exists\mathit{up}(r_{i})\colon r_{i}\in\mathit{rgn}_{1}\land r\in\mathit{up}(r_{i}))\land(\forall r^{\bullet}\in\mathit{rgn}_{1}\exists r^{\prime}\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\colon r^{\prime}\in\mathit{up}(r^{\bullet}))\land(\mathit{rgn}_{3}\cap\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn}_{1})=\emptyset)\land{}

\displaystyle(\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\exists\mathit{up}(r_{i})\colon r_{i}\in\mathit{rgn}_{2}\land r\in\mathit{up}(r_{i}))\land(\forall r^{\bullet}\in\mathit{rgn}_{2}\exists r^{\prime}\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\colon r^{\prime}\in\mathit{up}(r^{\bullet}))\land(\mathit{rgn}_{3}\cap\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn}_{2})=\emptyset)

From the construction of
\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn})
, the formula
\mathit{rgn}_{i}\cap\mathit{ngt}(\mathit{rgn}_{j})=\emptyset

is equivalent to the formula
\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{i}\neg\exists r^{*}\notin\mathit{rgn}_{j}\colon r\in\mathit{up}(r^{*})
.
Therefore:

\displaystyle(\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\exists\mathit{up}(r_{i})\colon r_{i}\in\mathit{rgn}_{1}\land r\in\mathit{up}(r_{i}))\land(\forall r^{\bullet}\in\mathit{rgn}_{1}\exists r^{\prime}\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\colon r^{\prime}\in\mathit{up}(r^{\bullet}))\land{}

\displaystyle(\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\neg\exists r^{*}\notin\mathit{rgn}_{1}\colon r\in\mathit{up}(r^{*}))\land{}

\displaystyle(\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\exists\mathit{up}(r_{i})\colon r_{i}\in\mathit{rgn}_{2}\land r\in\mathit{up}(r_{i}))\land(\forall r^{\bullet}\in\mathit{rgn}_{2}\exists r^{\prime}\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\colon r^{\prime}\in\mathit{up}(r^{\bullet}))\land{}

\displaystyle(\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\neg\exists r^{*}\notin\mathit{rgn}_{2}\colon r\in\mathit{up}(r^{*}))

Further, we only make use of

\displaystyle(\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\neg\exists r^{*}\notin\mathit{rgn}_{1}\colon r\in\mathit{up}(r^{*}))\quad\land\quad

\displaystyle(\forall r^{\bullet}\in\mathit{rgn}_{2}\exists r^{\prime}\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\colon r^{\prime}\in\mathit{up}(r^{\bullet})),

and the fact that
\mathit{rgn}_{1}
and
\mathit{rgn}_{2}
are distinct. Therefore,
\exists r^{\mathit{dist}}\colon r^{\mathit{dist}}\in\mathit{rgn}_{2}\land r^{\mathit{dist}}\notin\mathit{rgn}_{1}
(or vice versa).

By simplifying

\displaystyle(\exists r^{\mathit{dist}}\colon r^{\mathit{dist}}\in\mathit{rgn}_{2}\land r^{\mathit{dist}}\notin\mathit{rgn}_{1})\land{}

\displaystyle(\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\neg\exists r^{*}\notin\mathit{rgn}_{1}\colon r\in\mathit{up}(r^{*}))\land{}

\displaystyle(\forall r^{\bullet}\in\mathit{rgn}_{2}\exists r^{\prime}\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\colon r^{\prime}\in\mathit{up}(r^{\bullet})),

we obtain

\displaystyle(\exists r^{\mathit{dist}}\colon r^{\mathit{dist}}\in\mathit{rgn}_{2}\land r^{\mathit{dist}}\notin\mathit{rgn}_{1})\land{}

\displaystyle(\forall r\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\forall r^{*}\colon r^{*}\notin\mathit{rgn}_{1}\rightarrow r\notin\mathit{up}(r^{*}))\land{}

\displaystyle(\exists r^{\prime}\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\colon r^{\prime}\in\mathit{up}(r^{\mathit{dist}})),

which is clearly a contradiction, since
r^{\mathit{dist}}\notin\mathit{rgn}_{1}\land\exists r^{\prime}\in\mathit{rgn}_{3}\colon r^{\prime}\in\mathit{up}(r^{\mathit{dist}})
.

Finally, the marking
M_{F}
to be covered is constructed as
M_{F}=\mathbf{u}_{\mathit{fin}}
.
We have finished the construction of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}
, now we need to show that it
preserves the answer.

Claim 29
.

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\neq\emptyset
iff the marking
M_{F}
is coverable in
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

{claimproof}

(\Rightarrow)

Let
w\in(\Sigma\times\mathbb{D})^{*}
such that
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.
Moreover, assume that

\rho\colon c_{0}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{1}}_{t_{1}}}c_{1}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{2}}_{t_{2}}}\ldots\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{n}}_{t_{n}}}c_{n}

is an accepting run of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
.
We will show that there exists a sequence of firings

\rho^{\prime}\colon M_{\mathit{init}}[t^{\prime}_{\mathit{init}}\rangle~~M_{0}[t^{\prime}_{1}\rangle M_{1}[t^{\prime}_{2}\rangle\ldots[t^{\prime}_{n}\rangle M_{n}~~[t^{\prime}_{\mathit{fin}}\rangle M_{\mathit{fin}}

in
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
M_{\mathit{fin}}
covers
M_{F}
.
In particular, we construct the markings and transitions as follows:

•

M_{\mathit{init}}=\mathbf{u}_{\mathit{init}}
and
t^{\prime}_{\mathit{init}}=\langle\mathbf{u}_{\mathit{init}},\mathbf{u}_{q_{0}},\mathbf{id}\rangle
for
c_{0}=(q_{0},f_{0})
.

•

For all
0\leq i\leq n
with
c_{i}=(q_{i},f_{i})
, we set

M_{i}
as follows:

\displaystyle M_{i}={}

\displaystyle\{\mathit{init}\mapsto 0,\mathit{fin}\mapsto 0,q_{i}\mapsto 1\}\cup\{q\mapsto 0\mid q\in Q\setminus\{q_{i}\}\}\cup{}

\displaystyle\Big\{\mathit{rgn}\mapsto x\mid\mathit{rgn}\subseteq\mathbf{R},x=\big|\cap_{r\in\mathit{rgn}}f_{i}(r)\big|\Big\}

Furthermore,
t^{\prime}_{i}=\gamma(t_{i},\mathit{rgn}_{g})
where
\mathit{rgn}_{g}=\{r\mid d_{i}\in f_{i-1}(r)\}
.

•

M_{\mathit{fin}}
is as follows:

\displaystyle M_{\mathit{fin}}={}

\displaystyle\{\mathit{init}\mapsto 0,\mathit{fin}\mapsto 1\}\cup\{q\mapsto 0\mid q\in Q\}\cup\{\mathit{rgn}\mapsto M_{n}(\mathit{rgn})\mid\mathit{rgn}\subseteq\mathbf{R}\}

and
t^{\prime}_{\mathit{fin}}=\langle\mathbf{u}_{q_{n}},\mathbf{u}_{\mathit{fin}},\mathbf{id}\rangle
for
c_{n}=(q_{n},f_{n})
.

Note that
M_{F}
is covered by
M_{\mathit{fin}}
.

We can now show by induction that
\rho^{\prime}
is valid, i.e., all firings are
enabled and respect the transition relation.

(\Leftarrow)

Let
\rho\colon M_{\mathit{init}}[t_{0}\rangle~~M_{0}[t_{1}\rangle M_{1}[t_{2}\rangle\ldots[t_{n}\rangle M_{n}~~[t_{\mathit{fin}}\rangle M_{\mathit{fin}}
be a run of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}

such that
M_{\mathit{fin}}\geq M_{F}
, where
M_{F}
is the final marking.
We show that there exists a sequence of transitions

\rho^{\prime}\colon c_{0}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{1}}_{t^{\prime}_{1}}}c_{1}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{2}}_{t^{\prime}_{2}}}\ldots\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{n}}_{t^{\prime}_{n}}}c_{n}

in
\mathcal{A}
on
w=w_{1}w_{2}\ldots w_{n}
, such that
c_{n}
is a final
configuration, and, therefore,
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.
First, we notice the following easy to see invariant of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}
, which
holds for every
M_{i}
:

\sum_{p\in Q\cup\{\mathit{init},\mathit{fin}\}}\hskip-14.22636ptM_{i}(p)=1

(8)

i.e., there is always exactly one token in any of the places in
Q\cup\{\mathit{init},\mathit{fin}\}
.

Let us now construct
\rho^{\prime}
as follows:

•

c_{0}=(q_{0},f_{0})
is constructed such that
q_{0}
is picked to be
the state
q_{0}\in Q
with
M_{0}(q_{0})=1
(this is well defined due
to
\cref
eq:net_sim_invariant).

•

For all
1\leq i\leq n
, the transition
t^{\prime}_{i}
is picked to be
the transition such that
t_{i}\in\gamma(t^{\prime}_{i},\mathit{rgn}_{g})
for
some region
\mathit{rgn}_{g}
.
The data value
d_{i}
of
w_{i}
is then chosen to be compatible with the
guard of
\mathit{rgn}_{g}
, i.e.,
d_{i}\in\cap_{r\in\mathit{rgn}_{g}}f_{i-1}(r)
and
d_{i}\notin\cup_{r\in\mathbf{R}\setminus\mathit{rgn}_{g}}f_{i-1}(r)
.

It can then be shown by induction that, for all
0\leq i<n
, the
following holds:

(a)

c_{i}=(q_{i},f_{i})
where
q_{i}
is the (exactly one) state such that

M_{i}(q_{i})=1
and

(b)

the transition
t_{i+1}
is enabled.

We can then conclude that, since the last firing in
\rho
was
M_{n}[t_{\mathit{fin}}\rangle M_{\mathit{fin}}
, then, from the construction of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}
, it
holds that
M_{n}(q_{f})=1
for
q_{f}\in F
and so
\rho^{\prime}
is accepting.

\claimqedhere

\cref

lem:rsa_emptiness_preserves_answer and the observation that
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}

is single-exponentially larger than
\mathcal{A}
conclude the proof (
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
is
closed under primitive-recursive reductions).
∎

Lemma 30
.

The emptiness problem for
\mathrm{RsA}
is
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-hard.

Proof.

The proof is based on a reduction of coverability in TPNs (which is

\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-complete) to non-emptiness of
\mathrm{RsA}
s.
Intuitively, given a TPN
\mathcal{N}
, we will construct the
\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}

simulating
\mathcal{N}
, which will have the following structure:

•

There will be the state
q_{\mathit{main}}
, which will be active before and after
simulating the firing of TPN transitions.

•

Each place of
\mathcal{N}
will be simulated by a register of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}
;
every token of
\mathcal{N}
will be simulated by a unique data value.

•

For every TPN transition,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}
will contain a
gadget

that transfers data values between the registers representing the places
active in the TPN transition.
The gadget will start in
q_{\mathit{main}}
and end also in
q_{\mathit{main}}
.

•

Coverability of a marking will be simulated by another gadget connected
to
q_{\mathit{main}}
that will try to remove the number of tokens given in the
marking from the respective places and arrive at the single final
state
q_{\mathit{fin}}
.

Formally, let
\mathcal{N}=(P,T,M_{0})
with
P=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
be a TPN.
W.l.o.g. we can assume that
M_{0}
contains a single token in the place
p_{1}
,
i.e.,
M_{0}=\{p_{1}\mapsto 1,p_{2}\mapsto 0,\ldots,p_{n}\mapsto 0\}
.
We will show how to construct the
\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta,\{q_{\mathit{init}}\},\{q_{\mathit{fin}}\})
over the unary alphabet
\Sigma=\{a\}
such that
a marking
M_{f}
is coverable in
\mathcal{N}
iff the language of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}
is
non-empty.
The set of registers of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}
will be the set
\mathbf{R}=\{r_{\mathit{in}},r_{\mathit{tmp}}\}\cup\{r_{p},r_{p^{\prime}}\mid p\in P\}
.

{subfigure}

[b]0.43

q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\in r_{p}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{\mathit{in}}\leftarrow\{\mathit{in}\}\\
r_{\mathit{tmp}}\leftarrow\emptyset\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\in r_{p}\\
\mathit{in}\notin r_{\mathit{in}}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{\mathit{tmp}}\leftarrow r_{\mathit{tmp}}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{p}\leftarrow r_{\mathit{tmp}}\end{array}\end{array}

The
\textsc{LossyRm}(p)
protogadget.

q_{1}
q_{2}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{p_{1}}\leftarrow\emptyset\\
r_{p_{2}}\leftarrow r_{p_{1}}\cup r_{p_{2}}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 10:
The
\textsc{Move}(p_{1},p_{2})
protogadget.

{subfigure}

q_{1}
q_{2}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\{\mathit{in}\notin r\}_{r\in\mathbf{R}}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{p}\leftarrow r_{p}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}\\
\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 11:
The
\textsc{NewToken}(p)
protogadget.

Figure 12:
Protogadgets used in the construction of
\mathrm{RsA}_{\mathcal{N}}
.

Let us now define the following
protogadgets
, which we will later use
for creating a
gadget
for each TPN transition and a gadget for doing the
coverability test.
We define the following protogadgets:

1.

The
Lossy Removal
protogadget, which simulates a (lossy)
removal of one token from a place
p
is the
\mathrm{RsA}
defined as

\textsc{LossyRm}(p)=(\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\},\mathbf{R},\Delta^{\prime},\{q_{1}\},\{q_{3}\})

where
\Delta^{\prime}
contains the following three transitions (cf.
\cref
fig:protogadget_lossyrem):

\Delta^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}q_{1}\text{\small{\hbox to125.9pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 62.9498pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

(9)

Intuitively, the protogadget stores the data value
to be removed from
p
in a special register
r_{\mathit{in}}
.
Next, it simulates the calculation of the
difference of
r_{p}
and
r_{\mathit{in}}
.
This is done by accumulating the values
which are present in
r_{p}
and are not present in

r_{\mathit{in}}
into
r_{\mathit{tmp}}
.

Since some values may get “lost”, and disappear because of not being added to
the accumulated difference, this protogadget is
considered
lossy
.

2.

The

protogadget, which simulates
moving
all tokens
from a place
p_{1}
to a place
p_{2}

\mathrm{RsA}

in
\cref

p_{1}

p_{2}
in union with its value.

Intuitively, the protogadget adds the unique data
value from the input tape into the register
representing the place
p
. The uniqueness
of the data value is ensured by the
g^{\notin}
,
which requires that the
\mathit{in}
does not belong to
any of the registers from
\mathbf{R}
.

For convenience, we will use the following notation.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{1}=(Q_{1},\mathbf{R},\Delta_{1},\{q^{I}_{1}\},\{q^{F}_{1}\})
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}=(Q_{2},\mathbf{R},\Delta_{2},\{q^{I}_{2}\},\{q^{F}_{2}\})
be a pair of
\mathrm{RsA}
s with a single
initial state and a single final state.
We will use
\mathcal{A}_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{A}_{2}
to denote the
\mathrm{RsA}

(Q_{1}\uplus Q_{2},\mathbf{R},\Delta_{1}\cup\{q^{F}_{1}\text{\small{\hbox to54.64pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 27.31758pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}q^{I}_{2}\}\cup\Delta_{2},\{q^{I}_{1}\},\{q^{F}_{2}\})
.
Moreover, for
n\in{\rm Nature}_{0}
, we use
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{[n]}
to denote the

\mathrm{RsA}
defined inductively as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}^{[0]}={}

\displaystyle(\{q\},\mathbf{R},\emptyset,\{q\},\{q\}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}^{[i+1]}={}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}^{[i]}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{A}_{1}.

Intuitively,
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{[n]}
is a concatenation of
n
copies of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

For each TPN transition
t=\langle\mathit{In},\mathit{Out},\mathit{Transfer}\rangle
, we then create
the
gadget

\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}_{t}
in several steps.

1.

First, we transform
\mathit{In}
into the
\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{In}}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{In}(p_{1})}\mathbin{\cdot}\ldots\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{In}(p_{n})}
where
every
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{In}(p_{i})}
is defined as
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{In}(p_{i})}=\textsc{LossyRm}(p_{i})^{[\mathit{In}(p_{i})]}
, i.e., it is a concatenation
of
\mathit{In}(p_{i})
copies of
\textsc{LossyRm}(p_{i})
.

2.

Second, from
\mathit{Out}
we create the
\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Out}}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Out}(p_{1})}\mathbin{\cdot}\ldots\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Out}(p_{n})}

with
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Out}(p_{i})}
defined as
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Out}(p_{i})}=\textsc{NewToken}(p_{i})^{[\mathit{Out}(p_{i})]}
, i.e., it is
a concatenation of
\mathit{Out}(p_{i})
copies of
\textsc{NewToken}(p_{i})
.

3.

Third, from
\mathit{Transfer}
we obtain the
\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Transfer}}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Transfer}(p_{1})}\mathbin{\cdot}\ldots\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Transfer}(p_{n})}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{unprime}(p_{1})}\mathbin{\cdot}\ldots\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{unprime}(p_{n})}
such
that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Transfer}(p_{i})}=\textsc{Move}(r_{p_{i}},r_{p_{j}^{\prime}})
with
p_{j}=\mathit{Transfer}(p_{i})
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{unprime}(p_{i})}=\textsc{Move}(p^{\prime}_{i},p_{i})
.
Intuitively,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Transfer}}
first moves the contents of all registers
according to
\mathit{Transfer}
to primed instances of the target registers (in
order to avoid mix-up) and then unprimes the register names.

4.

Finally, we combine the
\mathrm{RsA}
s created above into the single gadget

\mathcal{A}_{t}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{In}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Transfer}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{Out}}
.

The initial marking will be encoded by a gadget that puts one new data value in
the register representing the place
p_{1}
.
For this, we construct the
\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}_{M_{0}}=\textsc{NewToken}(p_{1})
and
rename its initial state to
q_{\mathit{init}}
.

The last ingredient we need is to create a gadget that will encode the
marking
M_{f}
, whose coverability we are checking.
For this, we construct the gadget
\mathcal{A}_{M_{f}}=\mathcal{A}_{M_{f}(p_{1})}\mathbin{\cdot}\ldots\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{A}_{M_{f}(p_{n})}
where every
\mathcal{A}_{M_{f}(p_{i})}
is defined as

\mathcal{A}_{M_{f}(p_{i})}=\textsc{LossyRm}(p_{i})^{[M_{f}(p_{i})]}
, i.e., it is
a concatenation of
M_{f}(p_{i})
copies of
\textsc{LossyRm}(p_{i})
.
We rename the final state of
\mathcal{A}_{M_{f}}
to
q_{\mathit{fin}}
.
W.l.o.g. we assume that the set of states of all constructed gadgets are
pairwise disjoint.

We can now finalize the construction:

\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}
is obtained as the union of the following
\mathrm{RsA}
s:

\mathcal{A}_{M_{0}}=(Q_{M_{0}},\mathbf{R},\Delta_{M_{0}},\{q_{\mathit{init}}\},\{q^{F}_{M_{0}}\})
,
\mathcal{A}_{M_{f}}=(Q_{M_{f}},\mathbf{R},\Delta_{M_{f}},\{q^{I}_{M_{f}}\},\{q_{\mathit{fin}}\})
,
and
\mathcal{A}_{t}=(Q_{t},\mathbf{R},\Delta_{t},\{q^{I}_{t}\},\{q^{F}_{t}\})
for every
t\in T
, connected to the state
q_{\mathit{main}}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta,\{q_{\mathit{init}}\},\{q_{\mathit{fin}}\})
where

•

Q=\{q_{\mathit{main}}\}\cup Q_{M_{0}}\cup Q_{M_{f}}\cup\bigcup_{t\in T}Q_{t}
and

•

\Delta=\Delta_{M_{0}}\cup\Delta_{M_{f}}\cup\{q^{F}_{M_{0}}\text{\small{\hbox to54.64pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 27.31758pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

Claim 31
.

The marking
M_{F}
is coverable in
\mathcal{N}
iff
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}})\neq\emptyset
.

{claimproof}

(\Rightarrow)

Let there be the following run of
\mathcal{N}
:

\rho\colon M_{0}[t_{1}\rangle M_{1}[t_{2}\rangle\ldots[t_{n}\rangle M_{n}

such that
M_{n}
covers
M_{F}
.
We will show that there exists a word
w\in(\Sigma\times\mathbb{D})^{*}

and a run

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}\colon

\displaystyle c_{(\mathit{init},0)}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{1}}_{t^{\prime}_{(\mathit{init},1)}}}c_{(\mathit{init},1)}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{2}}_{t^{\prime}_{(\mathit{init},2)}}}\ldots c_{(\mathit{init},k_{\mathit{init}})}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{0}}}_{t^{\prime}_{(0,0)}}}\hskip 71.13188pt\lbag\textrm{initialization}\rbag

\displaystyle c_{(0,0)}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{0}+1}}_{t^{\prime}_{(0,1)}}}c_{(0,1)}\ldots c_{(0,k_{1})}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{1}}}_{t^{\prime}_{(1,0)}}}c_{(1,0)}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{1}+1}}_{t^{\prime}_{(1,1)}}}c_{(1,1)}\ldots c_{(1,k_{1})}\ldots c_{(n-1,k_{n-1})}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{n}}}_{t^{\prime}_{(n,0)}}}

\displaystyle c_{(n,0)}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{n}+1}}_{t^{\prime}_{(\mathit{fin},1)}}}c_{(\mathit{fin},1)}\ldots\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{\mathit{fin}}}}_{t^{\prime}_{(\mathit{fin},k_{\mathit{fin}})}}}c_{(\mathit{fin},k_{\mathit{fin}})}\hskip 116.6563pt\lbag\textrm{finalization}\rbag

of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}
on
w
such that
q\in F
for
c_{(\mathit{fin},k_{\mathit{fin}})}=(q,\cdot)
.
The run
\rho^{\prime}
will be constructed to preserve the following invariant
for each
0\leq i\leq n
:

c_{(i,0)}=(q_{\mathit{main}},f_{i})\quad\text{such that}\quad\forall p\in P\colon|f_{i}(r_{p})|=M_{i}(p).

(10)

Therefore, configurations with state
q_{\mathit{main}}
represent the TPN’s state
after (or before) firing a transition.
Firing a transition
t
is simulated by going to the gadget for
t

in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}
and picking input data values such that the run returns
to
q_{\mathit{main}}
in as many steps as possible (this is to take the run through
the
LossyRm
protogadgets that preserves the precise value of
the marking).
By induction on
0\leq i\leq n
, we can show that the
invariant in
\cref
eq:net_invariant is preserved (the base case is proved
by observing that the
initialization
part is correct).

(\Leftarrow)

Let
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}})
and

\displaystyle\rho\colon

\displaystyle c_{(\mathit{init},0)}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{1}}_{t^{\prime}_{(\mathit{init},1)}}}c_{(\mathit{init},1)}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{2}}_{t^{\prime}_{(\mathit{init},2)}}}\ldots c_{(\mathit{init},k_{\mathit{init}})}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{0}}}_{t^{\prime}_{(0,0)}}}\hskip 71.13188pt\lbag\textrm{initialization}\rbag

\displaystyle c_{(0,0)}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{0}+1}}_{t^{\prime}_{(0,1)}}}c_{(0,1)}\ldots c_{(0,k_{1})}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{1}}}_{t^{\prime}_{(1,0)}}}c_{(1,0)}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{1}+1}}_{t^{\prime}_{(1,1)}}}c_{(1,1)}\ldots c_{(1,k_{1})}\ldots c_{(n-1,k_{n-1})}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{n}}}_{t^{\prime}_{(n,0)}}}

\displaystyle c_{(n,0)}\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{n}+1}}_{t^{\prime}_{(\mathit{fin},1)}}}c_{(\mathit{fin},1)}\ldots\mathrel{\vdash^{w_{i_{\mathit{fin}}}}_{t^{\prime}_{(\mathit{fin},k_{\mathit{fin}})}}}c_{(\mathit{fin},k_{\mathit{fin}})}\hskip 116.6563pt\lbag\textrm{finalization}\rbag

be an accepting run of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}
on
w
such that for each
0\leq i\leq n
, it holds that
c_{(i,0)}=(q_{\mathit{main}},\cdot)
—this follows from
the structure of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}
.
We will construct a run

\rho^{\prime}\colon M_{0}[t_{1}\rangle M_{1}[t_{2}\rangle\ldots[t_{n}\rangle M_{n}

where each
t_{i}
is the TPN’s transition corresponding to the gadget
which the corresponding part of
\rho
traversed.
For all
0\leq i\leq n
, the following invariant will hold:

\forall p\in P\colon|f_{i}(r_{p})|\leq M_{i}(p).

(11)

We note that the run
\rho
might not have been the “
most
precise
” run of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}
, so the markings in the TPN run
overapproximate the contents of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}
’s registers in
\rho
.
The invariant can be proved by induction.

\claimqedhere

\cref

lem:tpn_coverabilityu_preserves_answer and the observation that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}

is single-exponentially larger than
\mathcal{N}
(assuming binary encoding of the
numbers in
\mathcal{N}
) conclude the proof (
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
is closed under primitive-recursive reductions).
∎

From
\cref
lem:rsa-emptiness-in-fomega and

\cref
lem:rsa-emptiness-fomega-hard, we immediately obtain

\cref
thm:rsa-emptiness.

See
7

A.2
Proofs of Closure Properties

See
9

Proof.

The proofs for closure under union and intersection are standard: for two

\mathrm{RsA}
s
\mathcal{A}_{1}=(Q_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1},\Delta_{1},I_{1},F_{1})
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}=(Q_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2},\Delta_{2},I_{2},F_{2})
with disjoint sets of states and registers, the

\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}_{\cup}
accepting the union of their languages is obtained as

\mathcal{A}_{\cup}=(Q_{1}\cup Q_{2},\mathbf{R}_{1}\cup\mathbf{R}_{2},\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2},I_{1}\cup I_{2},F_{1}\cup F_{2})
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{A}_{\cap}
accepting their intersection is constructed as
the product

\mathcal{A}_{\cap}=(Q_{1}\times Q_{2},\mathbf{R}_{1}\cup\mathbf{R}_{2},\Delta^{\prime},I_{1}\times I_{2},F_{1}\times F_{2})
where

Correctness of the constructions is clear.

For showing the non-closure under complement, consider the
language
L_{\neg\forall\mathit{repeat}}
from
\cref
ex:lang_negallrep, which can be accepted by

\mathrm{RsA}
.
Let us show that for the complement of the language,
namely, the language

L_{\forall\mathit{repeat}}=\{w\mid\forall i\exists j\colon i\neq j\land\mathbb{D}[w_{i}]=\mathbb{D}[w_{j}]\}

where all data values appear at least twice, there is no
\mathrm{RsA}
that can
accept it.

Our proof is a minor modification of the proof of Proposition 3.2
in
[
16
]
.
In particular, we show that if
L_{\forall\mathit{repeat}}
were expressible using an
\mathrm{RsA}
,
then we could construct an
\mathrm{RsA}
encoding accepting runs of a Minsky
machine.
Since emptiness of an RsA is decidable (cf.
\cref
thm:rsa-emptiness) and
emptiness of a Minsky machines is not, we would then obtain a contradiction.

Let us consider a Minsky machine
\mathcal{M}
with two counters and instructions of the
form
(q,\ell,q^{\prime})
where
q
and
q^{\prime}
are states of
\mathcal{M}
and

\ell\in\{\mathtt{inc},\mathtt{dec},\mathtt{ifzero}\}\times\{1,2\}
is the
corresponding counter operation.
A run of
\mathcal{M}
is a sequence of instructions (which can be viewed as

\Sigma
-symbols) together with a data value
d\in\mathbb{D}
assigned to
every symbol.
The data values are used to match increments with decrements of the same
counter (intuitively, we are trying to say that “
each increment is
matched with a decrement
”, in order to express that the value of the counter
is zero).
For instance, consider the following run:

\begin{array}[]{c}(q_{1},\mathtt{inc}_{1},q_{2})\\
12\end{array}\hskip-5.69054pt\begin{array}[]{c}(q_{2},\mathtt{inc}_{1},q_{3})\\
42\end{array}\hskip-5.69054pt\begin{array}[]{c}(q_{3},\mathtt{dec}_{1},q_{3})\\
12\end{array}\hskip-5.69054pt\begin{array}[]{c}(q_{3},\mathtt{inc}_{2},q_{2})\\
17\end{array}\hskip-5.69054pt\begin{array}[]{c}(q_{2},\mathtt{dec}_{1},q_{1})\\
42\end{array}\hskip-5.69054pt\begin{array}[]{c}(q_{1},\mathtt{ifzero}_{1},q_{4})\\
7\end{array}

(12)

Here, the first increment of counter 1 is matched with the first decrement of
the counter (both having data value 12) and the second increment of counter 1
is matched with the second decrement of the counter (both having data
value 42).
Since all increments of the counter are uniquely matched with a decrement, the
test at the end is satisfied so
\mathcal{M}
would accept (we assume
q_{4}
is
a final state).
To can accept such words, we can construct an automaton that checks the
following properties of the input word:

1.

The first instruction is of the form
(q_{1},\cdot,\cdot)
for
q_{1}

being the initial state of
\mathcal{M}
.

2.

Each instruction of the form
(\cdot,\cdot,q_{i})
is followed by an
instruction of the form
(q_{i},\cdot,\cdot)
.

3.

All increments have different data values and all decrements have
different data values.

4.

Between every two
(\cdot,\mathtt{ifzero}_{i},\cdot)
instructions
(or between the start and the first such an
\mathtt{ifzero}_{i}
instruction),

(a)

every
(\cdot,\mathtt{dec}_{i},\cdot)
needs to be preceded by
an
(\cdot,\mathtt{inc}_{i},\cdot)
instruction with the same data
value and

(b)

every
(\cdot,\mathtt{inc}_{i},\cdot)
needs to be followed by
a
(\cdot,\mathtt{dec}_{i},\cdot)
instruction with the same data
value.

Properties
1
and
2
can
be easily expressed using an NFA and, therefore, also using a
\mathrm{DRsA}
.
Property
3
is easily expressible using an
\mathrm{RsA}

(in fact, using a
\mathrm{DRsA}
) that collects data values of increments and
decrements of each counter in registers (we need two registers for every
counter).
Property
4a
is also expressible using an
\mathrm{RsA}

(again, using a
\mathrm{DRsA}
) that collects the data values of decrements and
whenever it reads an increment, it checks whether it has seen the increment’s
data value before.

Let us now focus on Property
4b
.
The negation of this property would be “
there is an increment not
followed by a decrement with the same data value
”.
This negated property is essentially captured by the
language
L_{\neg\forall\mathit{repeat}}
and so it is expressible using
\mathrm{RsA}
(in fact, it
can be expressed by an
\mathrm{NRA}
with guessing; or by a simple
\mathrm{NRA}
provided
that we change the accepted language to be prepended by a sequence of data
values that will be used in the run, separated from the run by a delimiter).
Therefore, if an
\mathrm{RsA}
could accept the complement of
L_{\neg\forall\mathit{repeat}}
, i.e.,
the language
L_{\forall\mathit{repeat}}
, then we would be able to solve the emptiness
problem of a Minsky machine, which is a contradiction.
∎

See
10

Proof.

The proof of closure under union is the same as in the proof of

\cref
thm:rsa-closure with the exception that the results uses only registers

\mathbf{R}_{1}
(we assume
|\mathbf{R}_{1}|=n
): all references to registers

r\in\mathbf{R}_{2}
are changed to references to
f(r)
where
f\colon\mathbf{R}_{2}\to\mathbf{R}_{1}
is an injection.

To show non-closure under intersection, consider the two languages

\mathcal{L}^{A}_{n}=\{w\mid\forall i<n\colon\mathbb{D}[w_{i}]=\mathbb{D}[w_{|w|-i+1}]\}~\text{and}~\mathcal{L}^{B}_{n}=\{w\mid\forall n\leq i<2n\colon\mathbb{D}[w_{i}]=\mathbb{D}[w_{|w|-i+1}]\}

Intuitively,
\mathcal{L}^{A}_{n}
is the language of words where the first
n
data
values in the word are repeated (in the reverse order) at the end of the word
and
\mathcal{L}^{B}_{n}
is the language of words where the
(n+1)
-th to
2n
-th data
values are repeated (also in the reverse order) at the
2n
-th to
(n+1)
-th
position from the end.
Both languages can be expressed via
\mathrm{NRA}_{n}
, and therefore also via
\mathrm{RsA}_{n}
.
Their intersection is the language

\mathcal{L}^{AB}_{n}=\{w\mid\forall i<2n\colon\mathbb{D}[w_{i}]=\mathbb{D}[w_{|w|-i+1}]\}
, which is the same as
\mathcal{L}^{A}_{2n}
and clearly needs
2n
registers.

Non-closure under complement follows from
\cref
thm:rsa-closure (its proof uses
\mathrm{RsA}_{1}
).
∎

See

The proof of closure of
\mathrm{DRsA}
under union is standard.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
be two complete
\mathrm{DRsA}

and
\mathcal{A}_{2}=(Q_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2},\Delta_{2},I_{2},F_{2})
, and their sets of states and registers
are disjoint. The
\mathrm{DRsA}

\mathcal{A}_{\cup}
accepting the union of their
languages is obtained as

and
F^{\prime}=\{(q_{1},q_{2})\mid q_{1}\in F_{1}\lor q_{2}\in F_{2}\}
.

The construction of the
\mathrm{DRsA}

\mathcal{A}_{\cap}=

(Q_{1}\times Q_{2},\mathbf{R}_{1}\cup\mathbf{R}_{2},\Delta^{\prime},I_{1}\times I_{2},F^{\prime}_{\cap})
accepting the intersection of

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
is similar to the construction
of
\mathcal{A}_{\cup}
, with the exception of
F^{\prime}_{\cap}
, which is obtained as

F^{\prime}_{\cap}=F_{1}\times F_{2}
.

The complement of
\mathrm{DRsA}
is obtained in the standard way
by completing it and swapping final and non-final states.
Since the automaton is already deterministic, the
correctness of the construction is obvious.
∎

See
12

Proof.

The closure under complement is trivial (complete the
\mathrm{DRsA}
and swap final
and non-final states; no new register is introduced).

To show that
\mathrm{DRsA}_{n}
is not closed under intersection, we use languages

\mathcal{L}^{A}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}^{B}_{n}
from the proof of
\cref
thm:rsan-closure.
In particular, both these languages are in
\mathrm{DRA}_{n}
(the
\mathrm{DRA}_{n}
needs more
states than the corresponding
\mathrm{NRA}_{n}
because it cannot
guess
where
the final part of the word starts and needs to consider all posibilites,
making the
\mathrm{DRA}_{n}
exponentially larger).
Similarly as in the proof of
\cref
thm:rsan-closure, a
\mathrm{DRsA}
for the
intersection of the languages, the language
\mathcal{L}^{AB}_{n}
, would need at
least
2n
registers.

Non-closure under union follows from De Morgan’s laws.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for
\cref
sec:dra

See
14

Proof.

(\subseteq)

Let
w=\langle a_{1},d_{1}\rangle\ldots\langle a_{n},d_{n}\rangle\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.
Then there is an accepting run
\rho
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
, such that

\rho\colon(q_{0},f_{0})\mathrel{\vdash^{\langle a_{1},d_{1}\rangle}_{t_{1}}}(q_{1},f_{1})\mathrel{\vdash^{\langle a_{2},d_{2}\rangle}_{t_{2}}}\cdots\mathrel{\vdash^{\langle a_{n},d_{n}\rangle}_{t_{n}}}(q_{n},f_{n})

with
q_{n}\in F
.
Furthermore, let

\rho^{\prime}\colon((S^{\prime}_{0},c^{\prime}_{0}),f^{\prime}_{0})\mathrel{\vdash^{\langle a_{1},d_{1}\rangle}_{t^{\prime}_{1}}}((S^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{1}),f^{\prime}_{1})\mathrel{\vdash^{\langle a_{2},d_{2}\rangle}_{t^{\prime}_{2}}}\cdots\mathrel{\vdash^{\langle a_{n},d_{n}\rangle}_{t^{\prime}_{n}}}((S^{\prime}_{n},c^{\prime}_{n}),f^{\prime}_{n})

be the run of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
on
w
(
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is deterministic and complete,
so
\rho^{\prime}
is unique).
We will show that
\rho^{\prime}
is accepting, and so
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.

Let us show that for all
0\leq i\leq n
, the following conditions hold:

1.

q_{i}\in S^{\prime}_{i}
,

2.

\forall r\in\mathbf{R}\colon f_{i}(r)\neq\bot\implies f_{i}(r)\in f^{\prime}_{i}(r)
, and

3.

\forall r\in\mathbf{R}\colon c^{\prime}_{i}(r)=\begin{cases}0&\text{iff }f^{\prime}_{i}(r)=\emptyset,\\
1&\text{iff }|f^{\prime}_{i}(r)|=1,\\
\omega&\text{iff }|f^{\prime}_{i}(r)|\geq 2.\end{cases}

We proceed by induction on
i
:

–

i=0
: Since the (only) initial state of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is the
macrostate
(I,c_{0}=\{r_{i}\mapsto 0\mid r_{i}\in\mathbf{R}\})
(cf. Line
1
), and
q_{0}\in I
, then
condition
1
holds.
Moreover,
f_{0}(r)=\bot
for every
r\in\mathbf{R}
, so
condition
2
holds trivially, and so does
condition
3
(the
c^{\prime}_{i}
of all registers is initialised to
zero on Line
1
and all registers are initialised to
\emptyset

in a run of an
\mathrm{RsA}
).

–

i=j+1
: We assume the conditions hold for
i=j
.
Let there be the following transition from the
j
-th to the
(j+1)
-th
configurations of the runs
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
:

(q_{j},f_{j})\mathrel{\vdash^{\langle a_{j+1},d_{j+1}\rangle}_{t_{j+1}}}(q_{j+1},f_{j+1})

and

((S^{\prime}_{j},c^{\prime}_{j}),f^{\prime}_{j})\mathrel{\vdash^{\langle a_{j+1},d_{j+1}\rangle}_{t^{\prime}_{j+1}}}((S^{\prime}_{j+1},c^{\prime}_{j+1}),f^{\prime}_{j+1})

and let
t_{j+1}\colon q_{j}\text{\small{\hbox to81.76pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.8787pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

is enabled.

{claimproof}

Since transition
t_{j+1}
is enabled in configuration
(q_{j},f_{j})
, it
holds that

(a)

\forall r\in g^{=}\colon f_{j}(r)=d_{j+1}
and

(b)

\forall r\in g^{\neq}\colon f_{j}(r)\neq d_{j+1}
.

From (a) and the induction hypothesis (condition
2
),
we have that
\forall r\in g\colon d_{j+1}\in f^{\prime}_{j}(r)
, so the

g^{\in}
-part of
t^{\prime}_{j+1}
’s enabledness holds.
Proving the
g^{\notin}
-part (i.e., that
\forall r\in\mathbf{R}\setminus g\colon d_{j+1}\notin f^{\prime}_{j}(r)
) is more difficult.
We prove this by contradiction.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that there exists a register
r\in g^{\neq}
such that
d_{j+1}\in f^{\prime}_{j}(r)
(other registers in

\mathbf{R}\setminus g
do not need to be considered because they do
not affect the enabledness of
t_{j+1}
).
Because

(i)

f_{j}(r)\neq d_{j+1}
,

(ii)

f_{j}(r)\in f^{\prime}_{j}(r)
(from
condition
2
of the induction
hypothesis), and

(iii)

d_{j+1}\in f^{\prime}_{j}(r)
(from the assumption),

we know that
|f^{\prime}_{j}(r)|\geq 2
.
From condition
3
of the induction hypothesis,
it holds that
c^{\prime}_{j}(r)=\omega
.
But then, since there is a register
r\in g^{\neq}
such that

c^{\prime}_{j}(r)=\omega
,
\cref
alg:det would on Line
1

return
\bot
, which gives us contradiction.

Claim 33
.

The following holds:

1.

q_{j+1}\in S^{\prime}_{j+1}
,

2.

\forall r\in\mathbf{R}\colon f_{j+1}(r)\neq\bot\implies f_{j+1}(r)\in f^{\prime}_{j+1}(r)
, and

3.

\forall r\in\mathbf{R}\colon c^{\prime}_{j+1}(r)=\begin{cases}0&\text{iff }f^{\prime}_{j+1}(r)=\emptyset,\\
1&\text{iff }|f^{\prime}_{j+1}(r)|=1,\\
\omega&\text{iff }|f^{\prime}_{j+1}(r)|\geq 2.\end{cases}

{claimproof}

1.

Trivial.

2.

Follows from the induction hypothesis and the definition of
the update function
\mathit{up}^{\prime}
on
Lines
1
–
1
.

3.

Follows from the induction hypothesis and the definition of

c^{\prime}
on Line
1
.

\claimqedhere

This concludes the first direction of the proof.

(\supseteq)

Let
w=\langle a_{1},d_{1}\rangle\ldots\langle a_{n},d_{n}\rangle\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.
Then there is an accepting run
\rho^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
on

, such that

.

\mathcal{A}
’s
configurations (i.e., pairs containing a state and assignment to registers)

, such that
\forall 1\leq i\leq n\colon U_{i}\in Q\times(\mathbf{R}\to\mathbb{D})

of
\mathcal{A}
over

U_{0}
contains a configuration
(q_{0},f_{0})
with

and

.

See
15

Proof.

(a)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
\mathrm{NRA}^{=}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{r}
be its register-local
version.
Because
\mathcal{A}_{r}
contains no disequality guards, the only way how

\cref
alg:det could fail is at Line
1
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
used at most one register
r
, then each state
q
of
\mathcal{A}_{r}

will also use at most one register
r_{q}
(the copy of
r
for
q
).
Then
P
on Line
1
will only be a set of elements with no
dependency.
For such a set, the Cartesian abstraction is precise, so the test on
Line
1
will never cause an abort.

(b)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathrm{URA}^{=}_{1}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{L}
.

\cref

See
17

Proof.

Let
L\in\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{NRA}_{1})
and
t
be a term describing
L

using unions, intersections, and complements, with atoms being
\mathrm{NRA}^{=}_{1}

languages.
W.l.o.g., we can assume that
t
is in the negation normal form (i.e.,
complements are only over atoms—it is easy to transform any term into this
form using De Morgan’s laws and double-complement elimination).

We can construct a
\mathrm{DRsA}

\mathcal{A}_{D}
accepting
L
inductively as
follows:

1.

Non-complemented literals
: if the literal is not complemented,
we use
\cref
thm:nra1-to-drsa(a) to obtain a
\mathrm{DRsA}
accepting it.

2.

Complemented literals
: first, we use

\cref
lem:complement_ura_nra to obtain the
\mathrm{URA}^{=}_{1}
accepting the
complemented language and then use
\cref
thm:nra1-to-drsa(b) to convert
it into a
\mathrm{DRsA}
.

3.

Unions and intersections
: we simply use

\cref
thm:drsa-closure to obtain the resulting
\mathrm{DRsA}
.
∎

Appendix C
Other Examples

Example 34
.

Consider the language
L_{\mathit{disjoint}}

[
31
, Example 2.5]
of words
w=uv
where
u
and
v
are non-empty and the data values in
u
and
v
are
disjoint, i.e.,

L_{\mathit{disjoint}}=\big\{w\mid\exists u,v\in\Sigma\times\mathbb{D}\colon w=uv\land u\neq\epsilon\land v\neq\epsilon\land\forall i,j\colon\mathbb{D}[u_{i}]\neq\mathbb{D}[v_{j}]\big\}

For instance, the language contains the word
ddee
, but does not contain the
word
dede
(we only consider the data values here).
An
\mathrm{RsA}_{1}
accepting this language looks as follows:
q
s
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r\leftarrow r\cup\{\mathit{in}\}\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\notin r\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\mathit{in}\notin r\end{array}\end{array}

Intuitively, the
\mathrm{RsA}
stays in state
q
, accumulating the so-far seen values
in register
r
, and at some point, it nondeterministically moves to
state
s
, where it checks that no data value from the first part of the word
appears in the second part.

We note that
L_{\mathit{disjoint}}
can be accepted neither by any
\mathrm{ARA}
(intuitively,
the different threads in an
\mathrm{ARA}
cannot synchronize on the transition from
the first part to the second part of the word), nor by any
\mathrm{ARA}(\mathsf{guess},\mathsf{spread})

(which are strictly more expressive than
\mathrm{ARA}
s).
It also cannot be accepted by a
\mathrm{DRsA}
.
∎

Appendix D
More Details about Extensions

D.1
RsAs with Register Emptiness Test

An
\mathrm{RsA}
with register emptiness test
(
\mathrm{RsA}^{=\emptyset}
) is a tuple
\mathcal{A}_{E}=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta^{=\emptyset},I,F)
where

Q,\mathbf{R},I,F
are the same as for
\mathrm{RsA}
s and the transition relation
\Delta^{=\emptyset}
for
\mathrm{RsA}^{=\emptyset}
is defined as

\Delta^{=\emptyset}\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times(\mathbf{R}\to 2^{\mathbf{R}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}})\times Q
.

is such that

can move from state

to state

if the
\Sigma
-symbol at
the current position of the input word is
a
and the
\mathbb{D}
-symbol at the
current position is in all registers from
g^{\in}
and in no register
from
g^{\notin}
and all registers from
g^{=\emptyset}
are empty; the content of the
registers is updated so that
r_{i}\leftarrow\bigcup\{x\mid x\in\mathit{up}(r_{i})\}
.

Lemma 35
.

For every
\mathrm{RsA}^{=\emptyset}

\mathcal{A}
, there exists an
\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with
the same language.

Proof.

Proof is done by showing the construction of modified
\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

corresponding to any given
\mathrm{RsA}^{=\emptyset}

\mathcal{A}^{=\emptyset}
.

The modification of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
appears in the the structure of states,
where we code the information about the empty registers into the states themselves,
and modify the transition relation accordingly.
The information about the emptiness of registers is kept in a form of
binary vector, denoted
\mathbf{v}_{\emptyset}
, such that
\mathbf{v}_{\emptyset}[r_{i}]=0
iff

r_{i}=\emptyset
, and
\mathbf{v}_{\emptyset}[r_{i}]=1
otherwise.
s
Let
\mathrm{RsA}^{=\emptyset}
be a tuple
\mathcal{A}^{=\emptyset}=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta,I,F)

then the equivalent
\mathrm{RsA}
is a tuple

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=(Q^{\prime},\mathbf{R},\Delta^{\prime},I^{\prime},F^{\prime})
where

Q^{\prime}=Q\times\mathbf{v}_{\emptyset}
,

I^{\prime}=\{(q_{i},\mathbf{v}_{\emptyset}^{0})\mid q_{i}\in I\land\forall r_{i}\in\mathbf{R}:\mathbf{v}_{\emptyset}[r_{i}]=0)\}
,

F^{\prime}=\{(q_{f},\mathbf{v}_{\emptyset})\mid q_{f}\in F\}
,
and
\Delta^{\prime}=\{(q_{1},\mathbf{v}_{\emptyset}^{1})\text{\small{\hbox to69.34pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.67073pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

D.2
RsAs with Register Equality Test

An
\mathrm{RsA}
with register equality test
(
\mathrm{RsA}^{=r}
) is a tuple
\mathcal{A}_{Eq}=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta^{\mathit{=r}},I,F)
where

Q,\mathbf{R},I,F
are the same as for
\mathrm{RsA}
s and the transition relation
\Delta^{\mathit{=r}}
for
\mathrm{RsA}^{=r}
is defined as

\Delta^{\mathit{=r}}\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times(\mathbf{R}\to 2^{\mathbf{R}})\times(\mathbf{R}\to 2^{\mathbf{R}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}})\times Q
.

Theorem 36
.

The emptiness problem for
\mathrm{RsA}^{=r}
is undecidable.

Proof.

The proof is done by reduction from coverability in
Petri nets
with

inhibitor arcs
, which is an undecidable problem
[
34
]
.
Given a
\text{PN}_{I}

\mathcal{N}_{I}
with inhibitor arcs, we construct a corresponding

\mathrm{RsA}^{=r}

\mathcal{A}^{=r}_{I}
.
The process of construction of
\mathcal{A}^{=r}_{I}
follows the
reduction of TPN to
\mathrm{RsA}
in the proof of

\cref
lem:rsa-emptiness-fomega-hard of
\cref
sec:proof-rsa-emptiness.
The structure of the resulting automaton differs in

protogadgets
whose concatenation is used for construction of
gadgets

which make up the reduced
\mathrm{RsA}^{=r}
.

The following
protogadgets
are used in the reduction:

1.

The
EmptyEq
protogadget (depicted in
\cref
fig:protogadget_emptyeq),
which simulates the inhibitor arc leading from place
p
is an
\mathrm{RsA}^{=r}
defined
as:

Intuitively, the
EmptyEq
simulates a register
emptiness test. At first, the protogadget explicitly
assigns the value of empty set to the register
r_{e}

and then it compares its equality with the content of the register
representing the place in which the inhibitor arc
originates.

a token is added to the register representing the destination.
The guard ensures that the added value is not already present within the register,
so that the number of values actually increases.

Intuitively, for each arc originating in a place
p
and terminating in transition, respective number
of values has to be removed from the register representing place
p
.
Therefore on each
protogadget
of this kind, one value is removed from a register
representing the source place in a lossless manner. The quality of being lossless is necessary in order
to sustain the semantics of source PN. Otherwise, some transitions may be enabled even though
they were not in the source PN.

D.3
RsAs with Removal

An
\mathrm{RsA}
with

(
\mathrm{RsA}^{\mathit{rm}}
) is a tuple

\mathcal{A}_{R}=(Q,\mathbf{R},\Delta^{\mathit{rm}},I,F)
,
where
Q,\mathbf{R},I,F
are the same as for
\mathrm{RsA}
s and the transition relation

\Delta^{\mathit{rm}}
is defined as

\Delta^{\mathit{rm}}\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times 2^{\mathbf{R}}\times(\mathbf{R}\to 2^{\mathbf{R}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}})\times Q
.

Theorem 37
.

The emptiness problem for
\mathrm{RsA}^{\mathit{rm}}
is
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-complete.

Proof.

\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-hardness follows trivially from
\cref
thm:rsa-emptiness.
To prove membership in
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
, we can reuse the proof of

\cref
lem:rsa-emptiness-in-fomega, with the only difference being in the
definition of
\mathit{Out}
for a transition

r_{i}\in\mathbf{R}
, if
r_{i}\in\mathit{rm}
, then

r_{i}\leftarrow\bigcup\{x\mid x\in\mathit{up}(r_{i})\}\setminus\{\mathit{in}\}
, else
r_{i}\leftarrow\bigcup\{x\mid x\in\mathit{up}(r_{i})\}
.

Theorem 38
.

The emptiness problem for
\mathrm{RsA}^{\mathit{rm}}_{=\emptyset}
is undecidable.

Proof.

The proof is done by showing the reducibility from
reachability

in
Petri nets
with
inhibitor arcs
, which is an undecidable problem.

Given a
\text{PN}_{I}

\mathcal{N}_{I}
with inhibitor arc, we construct the
\mathrm{RsA}^{\mathit{rm}}_{=\emptyset}

\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}_{I}}
.
The structure of the
\mathrm{RsA}^{\mathit{rm}}_{=\emptyset}

\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}_{I}}
is similar to the structure of
\mathrm{RsA}

\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{N}}

in the proof of the
\mathbf{F}_{\omega}
-hardness of emptiness in
\mathrm{RsA}
,
which can be seen in
\cref
lem:rsa-emptiness-fomega-hard of
\cref
sec:proof-rsa-emptiness.

The only difference is in
gadgets
used for simulation of respective transition in
\text{PN}_{\mathit{I}}

and gadget for doing the reachability test.
These are created by concatenation of respective
protogadgets
, which are defined in the following way:

{subfigure}

[b]0.4

q_{1}
q_{2}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}r_{p}=\emptyset\\
\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 17:
The
\textsc{Empty}(p)
protogadget.

{subfigure}

[b]0.4

q_{1}
q_{2}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\{\mathit{in}\notin r\}_{r\in\mathbf{R}}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{p}\leftarrow r_{p}\cup\{\mathit{in}\}\\
\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 18:
The
\textsc{NewToken}(p)
protogadget.

{subfigure}

[b]0.4

q_{1}
q_{2}
\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c}\framebox{$a$}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{in}\in r_{p}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\hline\cr r_{p}\leftarrow r_{p}\setminus\{\mathit{in}\}\\
\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 19:
The
\textsc{TokenRem}(p)
protogadget.

Figure 20:
Protogadgets used in the construction of the
\mathrm{RsA}^{\mathit{rm}}_{=\emptyset}
for
\mathcal{N}_{I}
.
